Electromagnetic waves generated by a hybrid dieletric-plasmonic dimer Thanks: ∗ Department of Applied Mathematics, The Hong Kong Polytechnic University, Hong Kong SAR. Email: xinlin.cao@polyu.edu.hk Thanks: ∗∗ Nanjing Center for Applied Mathematics, Nanjing, 211135, People’s Republic of China. Email: gh.hsen@njcam.org.cn Thanks: ‡ RICAM, Austrian Academy of Sciences, Altenbergerstrasse 69, A-4040, Linz, Austria. Email: mourad.sini@oeaw.ac.at. This author is partially supported by the Austrian Science Fund (FWF): P 30756-NBL and P 32660
Xinlin Cao ∗ Ahcene Ghandriche ∗∗ and Mourad Sini ‡

Abstract
. We know that the electric field generated by a plasmonic nano-particle (with negative permittivity) is given as a polarization of the incident electric field. Similarly, the electric field produced by a dielectric nano-particle (with positive but high permittivity) is given as a polarization of the incident magnetic field. In this work, we demonstrate that a hybrid dimer—composed of two closely coupled nano-particles, one plasmonic and the other dielectric—can polarize both the incident electric and magnetic fields. Consequently, such hybrid dimers have the potential to modify both the electric permittivity and magnetic permeability of the surrounding medium. However, this dual modification occurs only when the two nano-particles share common resonant frequencies. We derive the asymptotic expansion of the fields generated by these hybrid dimers in the subwavelength regime for incident frequencies near their shared resonant frequencies.



2010 Mathematics Subject Classification
35R30, 35C20, 35Q60

1.
Introduction and the main result

1.1.
Motivation

The interaction of light with nanoscale materials has revolutionized the understanding and manipulation of electromagnetic fields at subwavelength scales, paving the way for transformative applications in photonics, sensing, and metamaterials, see
[
23
,
6
,
15
,
28
]
. Among the various nanostructures, hybrid dimers—composed of plasmonic and dielectric nano-particles represent a significant advancement due to their ability to interact with both the electric and magnetic components of incident electromagnetic fields. Indeed, plasmonic nano-particles, characterized by their negative permittivity at optical frequencies, generate intense localized electric fields through surface plasmon resonances, effectively polarizing the incident electric field, see
[
10
,
26
,
7
,
21
,
15
,
5
,
22
]
. In contrast, dielectric nano-particles with high positive permittivity are known for their low-loss interaction with the magnetic component of light, driven by displacement currents that produce magnetic dipole resonances, see
[
3
,
29
,
24
,
33
]
. When combined in a hybrid dimer, these nano-particles exhibit coupled electromagnetic responses, allowing simultaneous polarization of both the electric and magnetic fields. This unique dual interaction makes hybrid dimers a promising platform for engineering media with tailored electric permittivity and magnetic permeability
[
2
,
30
,
34
]
.

A critical feature of hybrid dimers is their ability to achieve resonant frequency alignment between the plasmonic and dielectric components. This resonance matching enhances their electromagnetic coupling, enabling the formation of hybrid modes with strong field localization and enhancement. Recent studies have demonstrated that these hybrid modes can lead to significant field amplification in the gap between nano-particles, often referred to as ”hot spots,” which are central to applications such as surface-enhanced Raman spectroscopy (SERS), nanoscale biosensing, and nonlinear optics
[
2
,
34
,
25
]
.

In this work, we explore the theoretical and computational aspects of hybrid dimer interactions in the subwavelength regime. By deriving the asymptotic expansion of the fields generated near the common resonant frequencies of plasmonic and dielectric nano-particles, we aim to elucidate the underlying mechanisms driving the dual polarization effects. This approach provides a robust framework for understanding how hybrid dimers can modify the electromagnetic properties of the surrounding medium, offering new insights into their role in advanced photonic devices.
Below, we list few advantages of using hybrid dimers over homogeneous dimers of the form plasmonic-plasmonic or dielectric-dielectric dimers.

(1)

Field Polarization Mechanisms by hybrid Dimers
.
These hybrid dimers combine the complementary nature of plasmonic (electric field-driven) and dielectric (magnetic field-driven) responses, creating dual polarization effects. The combination allows for both the electric permittivity
\epsilon
and magnetic permeability
\mu
modulation, which is unique to hybrid structures. In a next work, we will analyze with more details, the case when we have a cluster of such dimers, regularly arranged in a given bounded domain, and show how the generated effective medium is a modulation, by averaging, of both the permittivity and permeability offering a way how to design single or double negative electromagnetic media.

(2)

Field Localization and Enhancement
. Hybrid dimers exhibit enhanced field localization and polarization in the gap region, benefiting from the synergistic effects of plasmonic and dielectric components. Indeed, in the case of homogeneous dimers, as plasmonic-plasmonic, strong electric field enhancements are localized in the gap, but losses due to ohmic heating can dampen efficiency. Regarding dielectric-dielectric dimers, we have relatively moderate enhancement but with less ohmic loss in comparison to plasmonic counterparts. Therefore, using heterogeneous dimers might improve both the enhancement, as for plasmonics, and reduce the ohmic loss, as for dielectrics.

(3)

Potential applications
.
The ability of hybrid dimers to modulate both
\epsilon
and
\mu
enables applications in designing metamaterials with tunable refractive indices, broadband absorbers, and devices requiring simultaneous electric and magnetic field control. Homogeneous plasmonic-plasmonic dimers are widely used in SERS, photothermal therapy, and plasmonic sensing due to their strong electric field enhancements, while dielectric-dielectric dimers are ideal for applications requiring low loss, such as photonic waveguides and resonators with high-Q factors. Therefore, hybrid dimers can offers possibilities to be used in both the mentioned applications as they share the qualities and avoid their disadvantages, to some extent.

1.2.
Main results

Let
D_{1}
and
D_{2}
be two bounded and
C^{2}
-regular domains in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
and model
D:=D_{1}\cup D_{2}

to stand for a dimer composed of two nano-particles
D_{1}
and
D_{2}
. We assume that
D_{1}
is a dielectric nano-particle, namely its permittivity and permeability constants enjoy the following properties:

\epsilon_{r}^{(1)}\,:=\,\frac{\epsilon^{(1)}}{\epsilon_{0}}\quad\text{with}\quad Re\left(\epsilon^{(1)}\right)\gg 1\quad\text{and}\quad\epsilon_{r}^{(1)}\,=\,1\quad\text{outside}\quad D_{1},

while

\mu_{r}^{(1)}\,:=\,\dfrac{\mu^{(1)}}{\mu_{0}}\,=\,1
in the whole space
\mathbb{R}^{3}
. The nano-particle
D_{2}
is taken to be a plasmonic one, i.e. with a moderately contrasting relative permittivity
\epsilon_{r}^{(2)}\,:=\,\dfrac{\epsilon^{(2)}}{\epsilon_{0}}
, enjoying negative real values,

{\mathrm{Re\,}}(\epsilon_{r}^{(2)})\,<\,0\quad\text{and}\quad\epsilon_{r}^{(2)}\,\sim\,1\quad\text{with}\quad\epsilon_{r}^{(2)}\,=\,1\quad\text{outside}\quad D_{2},

and a permeability satisfying
\mu_{r}^{(2)}\,:=\,\dfrac{\mu^{(2)}}{\mu_{0}}=1
in the whole space
\mathbb{R}^{3}
. More details will be given later on the related quantities. The electromagnetic wave propagation, in the time-harmonic regime, with the presence of the dimer
D
satisfies

(1.1)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{llrr}Curl(E)-i\,k\,\mu_{r}\,H=0,&\mbox{in }\mathbb{R}^{3},\\
&\\
Curl(H)+i\,k\,\varepsilon_{r}\,E=0,&\mbox{in }\mathbb{R}^{3},\end{array}\right.

where the total field
(E,H)
is of the form
(E:=E^{Inc}+E^{s},H:=H^{Inc}+H^{s})
and the incident plane wave
(E^{Inc},H^{Inc})
is of the form

(1.2)

E^{Inc}(x,\theta,\mathsf{p})\,=\,\mathsf{p}\,e^{i\,k\;\theta\cdot x}\quad\text{and}\quad H^{Inc}(x,\theta,\mathsf{p})\,=\,\left(\theta\times\mathsf{p}\right)\,e^{i\,k\;\theta\cdot x},

with
\theta,\mathsf{p}\in\mathbb{S}^{2}
,
\mathbb{S}^{2}
being the unit sphere, such that
\theta\cdot\mathsf{p}\,=\,0
, as the direction of incidence and polarization respectively, and the scattered field
(E^{s},H^{s})
satisfies the Silver-Müller radiation condition (SMRC) at infinity

(1.3)

\sqrt{\mu_{0}\epsilon_{0}^{-1}}H^{s}(x)\times\frac{x}{|x|}-E^{s}(x)\,=\,\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{|x|^{2}}\right).

This problem is well-posed in appropriate Sobolev spaces, see
[
12
]
and
[
27
]
, and we have the following behaviors

(1.4)

E^{s}(x)\,=\,\frac{e^{ik|x|}}{|x|}\left(E^{\infty}(\hat{x})\,+\,\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{|x|}\right)\right),\quad\mbox{as}\quad|x|\rightarrow\infty,

and

H^{s}(x)\,=\,\frac{e^{ik|x|}}{|x|}\left(H^{\infty}(\hat{x})\,+\,\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{1}{|x|}\right)\right),\quad\mbox{as}\quad|x|\rightarrow\infty,

where
(E^{\infty}(\hat{x}),H^{\infty}(\hat{x}))
is the corresponding electromagnetic far-field pattern of (
1.1
) in the propagation direction
\hat{x}:=\frac{x}{|x|}
.

Next, we present the necessary assumptions on the model (
1.1
) to derive the main results.

Assumption 1
.

(1)

Assumption on the dimer. Suppose that each component
D_{m}
of
D
can be represented by
D_{m}=a{B}_{m}+{z}_{m}
with the parameter
a>0
and the location
{z}_{m}
, for
m=1,2
.
Denote

a:=\max\{\mathrm{diam}(D_{1}),\mathrm{diam}(D_{2})\}\quad\text{and}\quad d:=\mathrm{dist}(D_{1},D_{2}).

We take

(1.5)

d\,=\,\alpha_{0}\,a^{t},

with
t
such that
0\,<\,t\,<\,1
, and
\alpha_{0}
is a positive constant independent on
a
.

(2)

Assumptions on the permittivity and permeability of each particle.
Regarding the permittivity, we assume that

(1.6)

\eta(x)\,:=\,\begin{cases}\eta_{1}\,:=\,\epsilon_{r}^{(1)}\,-\,1\,=\,\eta_{0}\;a^{-2}&\text{if $x\in D_{1}$}\\
&\\
\eta_{2}\,:=\,\epsilon_{r}^{(2)}\,-\,1\,\sim 1\quad\text{with}\quad{\mathrm{Re\,}}\left(\epsilon_{r}^{(2)}\right)\,<\,0&\text{if $x\in D_{2}$}\end{cases},

where
\eta_{0}
is a constant in the complex plane independent on the parameter
a
, such that
{\mathrm{Re\,}}\left(\eta_{0}\right)\,\in\,\mathbb{R}^{+}
. Moreover, regarding the permeability
\mu_{r}^{(m)}
, we assume that
\mu_{r}^{(m)}\,=\,1,
for
m=1,2
.

(3)

Assumption on the shape of
B_{m}
.

(a)

Regarding the shape
B_{1}
.
Since

(1.7)

\mathbb{H}_{0}\left(\mathrm{div}\,=0\right)(B_{1})\equiv Curl\left(\mathbb{H}_{0}\left(Curl\right)\cap\mathbb{H}\left(\mathrm{div}\,=0\right)\right)(B_{1}),

where

\displaystyle\mathbb{H}_{0}\left(Curl\right)\cap\mathbb{H}\left(\mathrm{div}\,=0\right)(B_{1})\,

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\,\Bigg\{E\in\mathbb{L}^{2}(B_{1}),\;\text{such that}\;Curl\left(E\right)\in\mathbb{L}^{2}(B_{1}),

\displaystyle\qquad\mathrm{div}\,\left(E\right)\,=\,0,\text{in}\,B_{1},\;\text{and}\;\nu\times E\,=\,0\;\text{on}\,\partial B_{1}\Bigg\},

see for instance
[
4
, Theorem 3.17]
, then there exists
\phi_{n,m,B_{1}}\in\mathbb{H}_{0}\left(Curl\right)\cap\mathbb{H}\left(\mathrm{div}\,=0\right)(B_{1})
, such that

(1.8)

e_{n,m,B_{1}}^{(1)}\,=\,\nabla\times\phi_{n,m,B_{1}}\,\,\mbox{ with }\,\,\nu\times\phi_{n,m,B_{1}}\,=\,0\,\,\text{and}\,\,\nabla\cdot\phi_{n,m,B_{1}}\,=\,0,

where
e_{n,m,B_{1}}^{(1)}\in\mathbb{H}_{0}\left(\mathrm{div}\,=0\right)(B_{1})
is an eigenfunction, corresponding to the eigenvalue
\lambda_{n}^{(1)}(B_{1})
, related to the Newtonian operator
N_{B_{1}}(\cdot)
defined, from
\mathbb{L}^{2}(B_{1})
to
\mathbb{L}^{2}(B_{1})
, by
1
1

1

The Newtonian operator

is bounded from
\mathbb{L}^{2}(B_{1})
to
\mathbb{H}^{2}(B_{1})
.

N_{B_{1}}\left(E\right)(x)\,:=\,\int_{B_{1}}\frac{1}{4\,\pi}\,\frac{1}{\left|x-y\right|}\,E(y)\,dy,

i.e.,

N_{B_{1}}\left(e_{n,m,B_{1}}^{(1)}\right)\;=\;\lambda_{n}^{(1)}(B_{1})\;e_{n,m,B_{1}}^{(1)},\quad\text{in}\;B_{1}.

We assume
2

2

To reduce the length of the notation in the sequel, we eliminate the need to depend on the vector
\phi_{n,m,B_{1}}
with respect to multiplicity index

that for

(1.9)

\int_{B_{1}}\phi_{n_{0},B_{1}}(y)\,dy\,\otimes\,\int_{B_{1}}\phi_{n_{0},B_{1}}(y)\,dy\,\neq\,0\quad\text{for certain}\;n_{0}\in\mathbb{N},

and

\sum_{n}\,\frac{1}{\lambda_{n}^{(3)}(B_{1})}\,\int_{B_{1}}e_{n,B_{1}}^{(3)}(x)\,dx\,\otimes\,\int_{B_{1}}e_{n,B_{1}}^{(3)}(x)\,dx\,\neq\,0,

\nabla M_{B_{1}}(E)(x)\,:=\,\underset{x}{\nabla}\int_{B_{1}}\underset{y}{\nabla}\left(\frac{1}{4\,\pi\,\left|x\,-\,y\right|}\right)\cdot E(y)\,dy,

i.e.,

(1.10)

\nabla M_{B_{1}}\left(e_{n}^{(3)}\right)\,=\,\lambda_{n}^{(3)}(B_{1})\,e_{n}^{(3)},\quad\text{in}\,\;B_{1}.

(b)

Regarding the shape
B_{2}
.
For
B_{2}
, we assume that

\sum_{n}\int_{B_{2}}\phi_{n,B_{2}}(y)\,dy\otimes\int_{B_{2}}\phi_{n,B_{2}}(y)\,dy\neq 0,

where
\phi_{n,B_{2}}(\cdot)
satisfy
(\ref{Eq0404})
in
B_{2}
, and

\int_{B_{2}}e_{n_{\star},B_{2}}^{(3)}(x)\,dx\,\otimes\,\int_{B_{2}}e_{n_{\star},B_{2}}^{(3)}(x)\,dx\,\neq\,0,\quad\text{for certain}\;n_{\star}\in\mathbb{N},

where
e_{n,B_{2}}^{(3)}(\cdot)\in\nabla\mathcal{H}armonic(B_{2})
is an eigenfunction, corresponding to the eigenvalue
\lambda_{n}^{(3)}(B_{2})
, related to the Magnetization operator
\nabla M_{B_{2}}\left(\cdot\right)
.

To gain more information about the used spaces
\mathbb{H}_{0}\left(div\,=\,0\right)(B_{m}),\,\nabla\mathcal{H}armonic(B_{m})
, and the eigensystems that relate to the Newtonian operator
N_{B_{m}}(\cdot)
and the Magnetization operator
\nabla M_{B_{m}}(\cdot)
, with

, it is recommended that the readers refer to Remark

.

(4)

Assumption on the used incident frequency
k
. Define the vector Magnetization operator
\nabla{M}^{0}_{B_{2}}(\cdot)
as
(
2.2
). Under the Helmholtz decomposition of
\mathbb{L}^{2}
-space given by (
2.1
),
denote
(\lambda_{n}^{(3)}(B_{2}),e_{n}^{(3)})
as the corresponding eigen-system of
\nabla M^{0}_{B_{2}}(\cdot)
over the subspace
\nabla\mathcal{H}armonic
. There exist complex constants

and

, with

(1.11)

1\,-\,k^{2}\,\eta_{1}\,a^{2}\,\lambda_{n_{0}}^{(1)}(B_{1})\,=\,\pm\;c_{0}\;a^{h}\quad\text{and}\quad 1\,+\,\eta_{2}\,\lambda_{n_{*}}^{(3)}(B_{2})\,=\;\pm\;d_{0}\;a^{h},\;\penalty\ \penalty\ a\ll 1,

where
\lambda_{n_{0}}^{(1)}(B_{1})
is the eigenvalue corresponding to

in

, related to the Newtonian operator

, and
\lambda_{n_{*}}^{(3)}(B_{2})
is the eigenvalue to

in

\nabla M_{B_{2}}(\cdot)

The conditions
(\ref{def-eta})
and
(\ref{condition-on-k})
can be derived from the Lorentz model by choosing appropriate incident frequency

. Indeed, recall the Lorentz model for the relative permittivity that

(1.12)

\epsilon_{r}=1+\dfrac{k_{\mathrm{p}}^{2}}{k_{0}^{2}-k^{2}-ik\xi},

where
k_{\mathrm{p}}
is the plasmonic frequency,
k_{0}
is the undamped frequency resonance of the background and
\xi
is the damping frequency with
\xi\,\ll\,1
. The details are given in Section
7
.

Based on the above conditions, we are now in a position to state our main result.

Theorem 1.1
.

Let
Assumption
1
, on the problem (
1.1
)-(
1.2
)-(
1.3
), which is generated by the dimer
D
, be satisfied. Let
x
be away from
D
, then for
t,h\in(0,1)
such that

4\,-\,h\,-\,4t\,>\,0,

the scattered wave admits the following expression

(1.13)

E^{s}(x)\,=\,k^{2}\,\sum_{m=1}^{2}\,\left[\Upsilon_{k}(x,z_{m})\cdot\tilde{R}_{m}\,-\,\underset{y}{\nabla}\Phi_{k}(x,z_{m})\times\tilde{Q}_{m}\right]\,+\,\mathcal{O}\left(a^{\min(3;7-2h-3t;10-2h-7t)}\right),

and its far-field admits the following expansion

(1.14)

E^{\infty}(\hat{x})\,=\,\frac{k^{2}}{4\,\pi}\,\sum_{m=1}^{2}e^{-ik\hat{x}\cdot z_{m}}\,\left[\left(I\,-\,\hat{x}\otimes\hat{x}\right)\cdot\tilde{R}_{m}\,+\,i\,k\,\hat{x}\times\,\tilde{Q}_{m}\right]\,+\,\mathcal{O}\left(a^{\min(3;7-2h-3t;10-2h-7t)}\right).

Here,

\left(\tilde{Q}_{1},\tilde{R}_{1},\tilde{Q}_{2},\tilde{R}_{2}\right)
is the vector solution to the following algebraic system

(1.15)

\left[\begin{pmatrix}I_{3}&0&0&0\\
0&I_{3}&0&0\\
0&0&I_{3}&0\\
0&0&0&I_{3}\end{pmatrix}\,-\,\begin{pmatrix}0&0&\mathcal{B}_{13}&\mathcal{B}_{14}\\
0&0&\mathcal{B}_{23}&\mathcal{B}_{24}\\
\mathcal{B}_{31}&\mathcal{B}_{32}&0&0\\
\mathcal{B}_{41}&\mathcal{B}_{42}&0&0\end{pmatrix}\right]\cdot\begin{pmatrix}\tilde{Q}_{1}\\
\tilde{R}_{1}\\
\tilde{Q}_{2}\\
\tilde{R}_{2}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\frac{i\,k\,\eta_{0}}{\pm\,c_{0}}\,a^{3-h}\,{\bf P}_{0,1}^{(1)}\cdot H^{Inc}(z_{1})\\
a^{3}\,{\bf P}_{0,1}^{(2)}\cdot E^{Inc}(z_{1})\\
i\,k\,\eta_{2}\,a^{5}\,{\bf P}_{0,2}^{(1)}\cdot H^{Inc}(z_{2})\\
\frac{\eta_{2}}{\pm\,d_{0}}\,a^{3-h}\,{\bf P}_{0,2}^{(2)}\cdot E^{Inc}(z_{2})\end{pmatrix},

with

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{13}\,

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\,\frac{k^{4}\,\eta_{0}}{\pm\,c_{0}}\,a^{3-h}\,{\bf P}_{0,1}^{(1)}\cdot\Upsilon_{k}(z_{1},z_{2})

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{23}\,

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\,k^{2}\,a^{3}\,{\bf P}_{0,1}^{(2)}\cdot\nabla\Phi_{k}(z_{1},z_{2})\times

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{31}\,

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\,k^{4}\,\eta_{2}\,a^{5}\,{\bf P}_{0,2}^{(1)}\cdot\Upsilon_{k}(z_{2},z_{1})

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{41}\,

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\,\,\frac{k^{2}\,\eta_{2}}{\pm\,d_{0}}\,a^{3-h}\,{\bf P}_{0,2}^{(2)}\cdot\nabla\Phi_{k}(z_{2},z_{1})\times

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{14}\,

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\,\frac{k^{2}\,\eta_{0}}{\pm\,c_{0}}\,a^{3-h}\,{\bf P}_{0,1}^{(1)}\cdot\nabla\Phi_{k}(z_{1},z_{2})\times

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{24}\,

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\,k^{2}\,a^{3}\,{\bf P}_{0,1}^{(2)}\cdot\Upsilon_{k}(z_{1},z_{2})

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{32}\,

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\,\,k^{2}\,\eta_{2}\,a^{5}\,{\bf P}_{0,2}^{(1)}\cdot\nabla\Phi_{k}(z_{2},z_{1})\times

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{42}\,

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\,\frac{k^{2}\,\eta_{2}}{\pm\,d_{0}}\,a^{3-h}\,{\bf P}_{0,2}^{(2)}\cdot\Upsilon_{k}(z_{2},z_{1})

where
\Upsilon_{k}(\cdot,\cdot)
is the Dyadic Green’s kernel given by

(1.16)

\Upsilon_{k}(\cdot,\cdot)\,:=\,\frac{1}{k^{2}}\,\nabla\nabla\Phi_{k}(\cdot,\cdot)\,+\,\Phi_{k}(\cdot,\cdot)\,I_{3},

with
\Phi_{k}(\cdot,\cdot)
being the fundamental solution of the Helmholtz equation given by

(1.17)

\Phi_{k}(x,y)\,:=\,\frac{e^{i\,k\,\left|x\,-\,y\right|}}{4\,\pi\,\left|x\,-\,y\right|},\quad x\neq y,

I_{3}
is the identity matrix, and
{\bf{P}}_{0,i}^{(j)}
, for
i,j=1,2
, are the polarization tensors defined by

\displaystyle{\bf P}_{0,1}^{(1)}\,

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,\int_{B_{1}}\phi_{n_{0},B_{1}}(x)\,dx\otimes\int_{B_{1}}\phi_{n_{0},B_{1}}(x)\,dx,\quad{\bf P}_{0,1}^{(2)}\,=\,\sum_{n}\frac{1}{\lambda_{n}^{(3)}(B_{1})}\,\int_{B_{1}}e_{n,B_{1}}^{(3)}(x)\,dx\otimes\int_{B_{1}}e_{n,B_{1}}^{(3)}(x)\,dx,

\displaystyle{\bf P}_{0,2}^{(1)}\,

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,\sum_{n}\int_{B_{2}}\phi_{n,B_{2}}(x)\,dx\otimes\int_{B_{2}}\phi_{n,B_{2}}(x)\,dx,\,\quad{\bf P}_{0,2}^{(2)}\,=\,\int_{B_{2}}e_{n_{*},B_{2}}^{(3)}(x)\,dx\otimes\int_{B_{2}}e_{n_{*},B_{2}}^{(3)}(x)\,dx,

with
\phi_{n}(\cdot)
satisfying
(\ref{pre-cond})
and
e_{n}^{(3)}
fulfilling
\nabla M_{B_{2}}\left(e^{(3)}_{n}\right)\,=\,\lambda_{n}^{(3)}(B_{2})\,e_{n}^{(3)}
.

Moreover, if we further extract the very dominant term
\left(Q_{1},R_{2}\right)
, in
D
, from the solution to the algebraic system
(\ref{eq-al-D1})
, it leads to the following corollary.

Corollary 1.2
.

Let
x
be away from
D
, then for
t,h\in(0,1)
fulfilling the condition

4\,-\,h\,-\,4t\,>\,0,

there holds the following expansion for the scattered field

(1.18)

\displaystyle E^{s}(x)\,

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,\pm k^{2}a^{3-h}\left[\frac{\eta_{2}}{d_{0}}\Upsilon_{k}(x,z_{0})\cdot{\bf P}_{0,2}^{(2)}\cdot E^{Inc}(z_{0})\,-\,\frac{i\,k}{c_{0}}\,\eta_{0}\underset{y}{\nabla}\Phi_{k}(x,z_{0})\times{\bf P}_{0,1}^{(1)}\cdot H^{Inc}(z_{0})\right]

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}\left(a^{\min(3-h+t;\;3;\;10-2h-7t;\;9-3h-5t)}\right),

and the following expansion for the far-field,

(1.19)

\displaystyle E^{\infty}(\hat{x})\,

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,\frac{k^{2}}{\pm 4\pi}e^{-ik\hat{x}\cdot z_{0}}\,a^{3-h}\left[\frac{\eta_{2}}{d_{0}}\left(I-\hat{x}\otimes\hat{x}\right)\cdot{\bf P}_{0,2}^{(2)}\cdot E^{Inc}(z_{0})-\frac{\eta_{0}k^{2}}{c_{0}}\hat{x}\times{\bf P}_{0,1}^{(1)}\cdot H^{Inc}(z_{0})\right]

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}\left(a^{\min(3-h+t;\;3;\;10-2h-7t;\;9-3h-5t)}\right),

where
z_{0}
denotes the intermediate point between
z_{1}
and
z_{2}
, and
{\bf P}_{0,1}^{(1)}
and
{\bf P}_{0,2}^{(2)}
are given in Theorem
1.1
.

We conclude this subsection by mentioning the following compact formulas for rewriting the tensors
{\bf P}_{0,i}^{(j)}
, with
1\leq i,j\leq 2
, and their values for the particular case where the nano-particles are balls.

(1)

The tensor

{\bf P}_{0,1}^{(1)}\,:=\,\int_{B_{1}}\phi_{n_{0},B_{1}}(x)\,dx\,\otimes\,\int_{B_{1}}\phi_{n_{0},B_{1}}(x)\,dx.

reduces, under the particular case of
B_{1}
being the unit ball, i.e.,
B_{1}\equiv B(0,1)
, and
n_{0}\,=\,1
, to

(1.20)

{\bf P}_{0,1}^{(1)}\,=\,\frac{12}{\pi^{3}}\,I_{3}.

(2)

We have the compact form of the tensor

{\bf P}_{0,2}^{(1)}\,:=\,\sum_{n}\int_{B_{2}}\phi_{n,B_{2}}(x)\,dx\,\otimes\,\int_{B_{2}}\phi_{n,B_{2}}(x)\,dx\,=\,\int_{B_{2}}Q(x)\cdot\overset{1}{\mathbb{P}}\left(Q\right)(x)\,dx,

where
Q
is the matrix given by
(\ref{DefQ(x)})
.

(3)

We rewrite the tensor
{\bf P}_{0,1}^{(2)}
as

{\bf P}_{0,1}^{(2)}\,:=\,\sum_{n}\frac{1}{\lambda_{n}^{(3)}(B_{1})}\,\int_{B_{1}}e_{n,B_{1}}^{(3)}(x)\,dx\,\otimes\,\int_{B_{1}}e_{n,B_{1}}^{(3)}(x)\,dx\,=\,\int_{B_{1}}\,\nabla M_{B_{1}}^{-1}\left(I_{3}\,\chi_{B_{1}}\right)(x)\,dx.

In addition, under the particular case of
B_{1}
being the unit ball, i.e.,
B_{1}\equiv B(0,1)
, we obtain

{\bf P}_{0,1}^{(2)}\,=\,4\,\pi\,I_{3}.

(4)

Finally the tensor

{\bf P}_{0,2}^{(2)}\,:=\,\int_{B_{2}}e^{(3)}_{n_{\star},B_{2}}(x)\,dx\,\otimes\,\int_{B_{2}}e^{(3)}_{n_{\star},B_{2}}(x)\,dx.

reduces, under the particular case of
B_{2}
being the unit ball, i.e.,
B_{2}\equiv B(0,1)
, and
n_{\star}\,=\,1
, to

(1.21)

{\bf P}_{0,2}^{(2)}\,=\,\frac{4\,\pi}{27}\,I_{3}.

The computation details can be found in Subsection
7.2
. We observe that for the case of balls, the main tensors
{\bf P}_{0,1}^{(1)}
and
{\bf P}_{0,2}^{(2)}
are not vanishing and are proportional to the identity matrix.

1.3.
Discussion about the results

The estimation of the electromagnetic fields generated by a single type of nano-particles is already known in the literature, see
[
3
,
9
]
for dielectric nano-particles and
[
20
,
10
]
for plasmonic nano-particles. The related results correspond to those derived here by keeping only the block matrix given by
(\mathcal{B}_{13},\mathcal{B}_{23},\mathcal{B}_{31},\mathcal{B}_{41})
or
(\mathcal{B}_{14},\mathcal{B}_{24},\mathcal{B}_{32},\mathcal{B}_{42})
, respectively in
1.15
. The originality here is to have derived the fields generated by such hybrid dimers having not only different shape but also different contrasting materials. We call such hybrid dimer heterogeneous dimers while those dimers with similar, or same, scales are called homogeneous dimers.

The approximations of the electric fields in
1.13
and
1.14
are modeled by the vectors
Q_{1},Q_{2},R_{1}
and
R_{2}
which are solutions of the algebraic system
1.15
. Precisely,
Q_{j}
and
R_{j}
model the magnetic and electric poles of the nano-particles
D_{j}
,
j=1,2
, respectively. Inverting this algebraic system, using the Born series expansions, provides with a cascade of fields approximations where the most dominant field is described in Corollary
1.19
. In this corollary, we see that the generated electromagnetic field by the dimer is a combination of the electric pole generated by the plasmonic nano-particle and magnetic pole generated by the dielectric nano-particle. This shows how the dimer plays a role of dipole to generate the electromagnetic field. The higher order terms in the Born series describe the two types of contributions that worth mentioning.

(1)

The first class of contributions consist in the higher order terms modeling multipoles for each nano-particle, taken in isolation. Such higher order terms are also seen when deriving the expansion for single nano-particle.

(2)

The second class of contributions consist in the mutual interaction between the two nano-particles. These terms model the multiple scattering between the two nano-particles.

Based on this classification of the contributions, the mutual interaction between the two nano-particles, forming the heterogeneous dimer, is richer, as compared to single nano-particles or homogeneous dimers, as these contributions enter into game as combinations of higher order modes, for each nano-particle, with mutual interactions between the two nano-particles.

Such a mutual interaction between the two nano-particles is possible only because they are tuned to resonate at a common incident frequencies. Otherwise, we can also excite the dimer with frequencies away from the common resonances, but eventually near to resonances of one of the nano-particles, then the dimer will predominately behave as a single nano-particle.

As a plasmonic-dielectric dimer has the potential of generating both electric and magnetic polarizations, then we expect to use a cluster of such dimers to generate both effective electric permittivity and magnetic permeability. As we excite such systems with nearly resonating incident frequencies, we expect to be able to generate both single negative (permittivity or permeability) or eventually double negative (permittivity and permeability). Such investigation will be reported in a forthcoming work. Let us mention that the use of cluster of nano-particles (single nano-particle) to generate single negative permeability of single negative permittivity is already confirmed in
[
11
]
and
[
8
]
respectively.

The rest of the paper is organized as follows. In Section
2
, we introduce some preliminaries including the
\mathbb{L}^{2}
-Helmholtz decomposition and the Lippmann Schwinger system of equations for the solution to (
1.1
). Based on the Lippmann Schwinger system, we present the a-priori estimates first for single nano-particle and then for the dimer of nano-particles. The estimations for the related scattering coefficients, i.e. corresponding to the induced polarization tensors, are analyzed as well. In Section
3
, the precise form of the linear algebraic system is investigated. Section
4
is devoted to prove Theorem
1.1
and Corollary
1.2
, on the basis of the outcomes in Section
2
and Section
3
. In Section
5
, we provide the proof of all the a-priori estimates presented in Section
2
. In Section
6
, detailed analysis are given for the construction of the linear algebraic system presented in Section
3
. Section
7
, which will be given as an appendix, will be devoted to the justification of some mentioned results in Section
1
.

2.
Some preliminaries and a-priori estimates

In this section, we present some necessary preliminaries and significant a-prior estimates. For the preliminaries, we cite some key points here for the completeness of the paper, please refer to
[
9
]
for more details.

2.1.
\mathbb{L}^{2}(B)
-Helmholtz decomposition.

The following direct sum provides a useful decomposition of
\mathbb{L}^{2}(B)
-space, see
[
13
, Chapter IX, Table I, Page 314]
,

(2.1)

\mathbb{L}^{2}(B)\,=\,\mathbb{H}_{0}\left(\mathrm{div}\,=0\right)(B)\overset{\perp}{\oplus}\mathbb{H}_{0}\left(Curl=0\right)(B)\overset{\perp}{\oplus}\nabla\mathcal{H}armonic(B),

where

\displaystyle\mathbb{H}_{0}\left(\mathrm{div}\,=0\right)(B)

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\left\{E\in\mathbb{L}^{2}(B),\,\mathrm{div}\,E=0,\;\text{in}\,B,\;\;\nu\cdot E=0\,\;\text{on}\;\,\partial B\right\},

\displaystyle\mathbb{H}_{0}\left(Curl=0\right)(B)

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\left\{E\in\mathbb{L}^{2}(B),\,Curl\,E=0,\;\text{in}\,B,\,\nu\times E=0\,\;\text{on}\;\,\partial B\right\},

and

\nabla\mathcal{H}armonic(B)\,:=\,\left\{E:\;E=\nabla\psi,\,\psi\in\mathbb{H}^{1}(B),\,\Delta\psi\,=\,0,\;\text{in}\;B\right\}.

From the decomposition (
2.1
),
we define
\overset{1}{\mathbb{P}},\overset{2}{\mathbb{P}}
and
\overset{3}{\mathbb{P}}
to be the natural projectors as follows

\overset{1}{\mathbb{P}}:=\mathbb{L}^{2}(B)\longrightarrow\mathbb{H}_{0}\left(\mathrm{div}\,=0\right)(B),\qquad\overset{2}{\mathbb{P}}:=\mathbb{L}^{2}(B)\longrightarrow\mathbb{H}_{0}\left(Curl=0\right)(B)\quad\text{and}\quad\overset{3}{\mathbb{P}}:=\mathbb{L}^{2}(B)\longrightarrow\nabla\mathcal{H}armonic(B).

2.2.
Lippmann-Schwinger integral formulation of the solution.

For any vector field
F
, we define the Newtonian operator
N^{k}_{D}(\cdot)
and the Magnetization operator
\nabla M^{k}_{D}(\cdot)
as follows

(2.2)

N^{k}_{D}(F)(x):=\int_{D}\Phi_{k}(x,y)F(y)\,dy\quad\text{and}\quad\nabla M^{k}_{D}(F)(x):=\underset{x}{\nabla}\int_{D}\underset{y}{\nabla}\Phi_{k}(x,y)\cdot F(y)\,dy.

where
\Phi_{k}(\cdot,\cdot)
is the fundamental solution of the Helmholtz equation given by
(\ref{Helmholtz-kernel})
.
The solution to (
1.1
) of the integro-differential form can be formulated as the following proposition.

Proposition 2.1
.

The solution to the problem
(\ref{U})
satisfies

(2.3)

E(x)\,+\,\nabla M^{k}_{D}(\eta\,E)(x)-k^{2}\,N^{k}_{D}(\eta\,E)(x)\,=\,E^{Inc}(x),\quad x\in\mathbb{R}^{3},

where
\eta(\cdot)
is defined by (
1.6
).

Proof.

The proposition can be proved by utilizing the Stratton-Chu formula directly, see
[
12
, Theorem 6.1]
for more detailed discussions.
∎

Motivated by the study of the L.S.E given by
(\ref{LS eq2})
, on the sub-spaces involved in the
\mathbb{L}^{2}
-space decomposition, see for instance
(\ref{L2-decomposition})
, using spectral theory techniques, it is crucial for us to discuss the Magnetization operator with vanishing frequency, i.e.
\nabla M(\cdot)\,:=\,\nabla M^{0}(\cdot)
, and the Newtonian operator with vanishing frequency, i.e.
N(\cdot)\,:=\,N^{0}(\cdot)
, on the L.H.S of
(\ref{LS eq2})
. This idea is clarified by the following remark.

Remark 2.2
.

The case for a domain
D
being small leads to the expansions of the Magnetization operator
\nabla M^{k}_{D}(\cdot)
and the Newtonian operator
N^{k}_{D}(\cdot)
, defined in
(\ref{N-M opera})
as

(2.4)

\displaystyle\nabla M^{k}_{D}(F)(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\nabla M_{D}(F)(x)+\frac{k^{2}}{2}\,N_{D}(F)(x)+\frac{ik^{3}}{12\pi}\int_{D}F(y)dy-\frac{k^{2}}{2}\int_{D}\Phi_{0}(x,y)\frac{A(x,y)\cdot F(y)}{|x-y|^{2}}dy

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4\pi}\;\sum_{n\geq 3}\frac{(ik)^{n+1}}{(n+1)!}\;\int_{D}\;\underset{y}{\nabla}\underset{y}{\nabla}\left|x-y\right|^{n}\cdot F(y)\,dy,\quad x\in D,

and

(2.5)

N^{k}_{D}(F)(x)=N_{D}(F)(x)+\frac{ik}{4\pi}\int_{D}F(y)dy+\frac{1}{4\pi}\;\sum_{n\geq 1}\frac{(ik)^{n+1}}{(n+1)!}\;\int_{D}\;\left|x-y\right|^{n}F(y)\,dy,\quad x\in D,

where
A(\cdot,\cdot)
is the matrix given by
A(x,y):=\left(x-y\right)\otimes\left(x-y\right)
. For more details on the derivation of
(\ref{expansion-gradMk})
and
(\ref{expansion-Nk})
, we refer the readers to
[
9
, Section 2.2]
.

In addition to the above remark, the following behaviors of the Magnetization operator and the Newtonian operator on the sub-spaces involved in the
\mathbb{L}^{2}
-space decomposition, given by
(\ref{L2-decomposition})
, hold.

Remark 2.3
.

Two points are in order.

(1)

The Newtonian operator
N_{B}(\cdot)
projected onto the subspace
\mathbb{H}_{0}(\mathrm{div}\,=0)(B)

(
respectively,
\mathbb{H}_{0}(Curl=0)(B)
)
admits an eigensystem that we denote by
\left(\lambda^{(1)}_{n}(B);e^{(1)}_{n,B}\right)

(
respectively,
\left(\lambda^{(2)}_{n}(B);e^{(2)}_{n,B}\right)

)
. Besides, we have

(2.6)

\int_{B}e^{(j)}_{n,B}(y)\,dy=0,\;\forall\;n\,\in\mathbb{N}\;\text{and}\;j=1,2.

(2)

For the Magnetization operator, the following relations hold.

\ast

\nabla M^{k}_{B}(\cdot)
projected onto the subspace
\mathbb{H}_{0}(\mathrm{div}\,=0)(B)
is a vanishing operator, i.e.

(2.7)

\forall\;E\in\mathbb{H}_{0}\left(\mathrm{div}\,=0\right)(B)\;\text{we have}\;\nabla M_{B}^{k}(E)=0.

\ast

\nabla M^{k}_{B}(\cdot)
projected onto the subspace
\mathbb{H}_{0}(Curl=0)(B)
satisfy

(2.8)

\forall\;E\in\mathbb{H}_{0}\left(Curl=0\right)(B)\;\text{we have}\;\nabla M_{B}^{k}(E)\,=\,k^{2}\,N^{k}_{B}(E)\,+\,E\,\chi_{B},

where
\chi_{B}(\cdot)
is the characteristic function set.

\ast

\nabla M_{B}(\cdot)
projected onto the subspace
\nabla\mathcal{H}armonic
admits an eigensystem that we denote by
\left(\lambda^{(3)}_{n}(B);e^{(3)}_{n,B}\right)
.

For the existence and the construction of
\left(\lambda^{(j)}_{n}(B);e^{(j)}_{n,B}\right)_{n\in\mathbb{N}},j=1,2,3
, we refer to
[
20
, Section 5]
. More properties for the Magnetization operator, such as the self-adjointness, positivity, spectrum, boundedness, etc., can be found in
[
1
,
14
,
16
,
17
,
18
]
and
[
31
]
. Besides,
(\ref{RefNeeded1})
can be proved by using
(\ref{L2-decomposition})
and knowing that
I_{3}\in\nabla\mathcal{H}armonic
.

2.3.
A-priori Estimates.

Based on the decomposition (
2.1
), we present here some necessary a-prior estimates related to the electric total field
E
, derived from the Lippmann Schwinger equation (
2.3
), and some scattering coefficients, which play an important role in the proof of our main results. In order to achieve this, we will require an intermediate result that will clarify the total field estimates that can be derived from a single nano-particle, whether it’s dielectric or plasmonic. This is the subject of the following lemma.

Lemma 2.4

(Estimate for just one nano-particle)
.

Under
Assumption
1
, we consider the problem (
1.1
) with only one distributed nano-particle. Let
k
fulfill

k^{2}\,:=\,\frac{1\mp c_{0}\,a^{h}}{\eta_{1}\,a^{2}\,\lambda_{n_{0}}^{(1)}(B_{1})}\,\sim 1.

Then, for
h<2
, there holds

(1)

the electric field generated by a single dielectric nano-particle

\lVert\tilde{E}\rVert_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(B_{1})}\,=\,\mathcal{O}\left(a^{1-h}\right),

(2)

the electric field generated by a single Plasmonic nano-particle

\lVert\tilde{E}\rVert_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(B_{2})}\,=\,\mathcal{O}\left(a^{-h}\right),

and, regardless on the used nano-particle, we have

(2.9)

\overset{2}{\mathbb{P}}\left(\tilde{E}\right)\,=\,0.

Proof.

See Subsection
5.1
.
∎

Furthermore, in the sequel, we will frequently use certain notations that require clarification.

Notation
.

In the presence of a Dimer, we denote by
E_{m}(\cdot)
the restriction of
E(\cdot)
onto the nano-particle
D_{m}
, for
m=1,2
, i.e.
E_{m}(\cdot):=E|_{{D_{m}}}(\cdot)
. Besides, we denote by
\tilde{F}(\cdot)
the vector field that we obtain by scaling
F(\cdot)
, from
D
to
B
, i.e.,

\tilde{F}\left(\eta\right)\,=\,F(z\,+\,a\,\eta),\qquad\eta\in B.

Based on the estimates of the total electric field generated by the presence of a single nano-particle, whether it’s dielectric or plasmonic, see Lemma
2.4
, we develop the following proposition to estimate the total electric field generated by the presence of a dimer.

Proposition 2.5

(Estimation for the dimer)
.

Under
Assumption
1
, we consider the problem (
1.1
) for the dimer
D
. Let
k
fulfill

(2.10)

k^{2}:=\frac{1\mp c_{0}\,a^{h}}{\eta_{1}\,a^{2}\,\lambda_{n_{0}}^{(1)}(B_{1})}\,\sim 1.

Then for
t,h\in(0,1)
, there holds the estimation

(2.11)

\begin{cases}\left\lVert\overset{1}{\mathbb{P}}\left(\tilde{E}_{1}\right)\right\rVert_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(B_{1})}\,=\,\mathcal{O}\left(a^{1-h}\right)\qquad&\text{,}\qquad\left\lVert\overset{1}{\mathbb{P}}\left(\tilde{E}_{2}\right)\right\rVert_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(B_{2})}\,=\,\mathcal{O}\left(a^{\min(1;4-h-3t)}\right)\\
\\
\left\lVert\overset{3}{\mathbb{P}}\left(\tilde{E}_{1}\right)\right\rVert_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(B_{1})}\,=\,\mathcal{O}\left(a^{2}\right)\qquad&\text{,}\qquad\left\lVert\overset{3}{\mathbb{P}}\left(\tilde{E}_{2}\right)\right\rVert_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(B_{2})}\,=\,\mathcal{O}\left(a^{-h}\right)\end{cases}

In addition, we have

(2.12)

\overset{2}{\mathbb{P}}\left(\tilde{E}_{1}\right)\;=\;\overset{2}{\mathbb{P}}\left(\tilde{E}_{2}\right)\;=\;0.

Proof.

See Subsection
5.2
.
∎

In addition to estimating the electric total field generated by a dimer, which can be found in Proposition
2.5
, it’s necessary to estimate the scattering coefficients related to the problem
(\ref{U})
. The purpose of the following definition is to define the scattering coefficients related to the problem
(\ref{U})
, associated with the used dimer, which will be utilized to justify our derived results.

Definition 2.6
.

We define
W_{1},W_{2},V_{1}
and
V_{2}
to be solutions of,

(2.13)

\displaystyle\left(I+\eta_{1}\,\nabla M^{-k}_{D_{1}}\,-\,k^{2}\,\eta_{1}\,N^{-k}_{D_{1}}\right)\left(W_{1}\right)(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}(x,z_{1}),\quad\;\,\;\quad x\in D_{1},

(2.14)

\displaystyle\left(I+\eta_{2}\,\nabla M^{-k}_{D_{2}}\,-\,k^{2}\,\eta_{2}\,N^{-k}_{D_{2}}\right)\left(W_{2}\right)(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\overset{1}{\mathbb{P}}\left(\mathcal{P}(x,z_{2})\right),\quad x\in D_{2},

(2.15)

\displaystyle\left(I+\eta_{m}\,\nabla M^{-k}_{D_{m}}\,-\,k^{2}\,\eta_{m}\,N^{-k}_{D_{m}}\right)\left(V_{m}\right)(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle I_{3},\quad\;\;\qquad\qquad x\in D_{m},\quad m=1,2,

where, for
m=1,2
, the operators
\nabla M^{-k}_{D_{m}}(\cdot)
and
N^{-k}_{D_{m}}(\cdot)
are the adjoint operators to
\nabla M^{k}_{D_{m}}(\cdot)
and
N^{k}_{D_{m}}(\cdot)
, introduced in (
2.2
), and
\mathcal{P}(x,z)
is the matrix expressed by

\mathcal{P}(x,z)\,:=\,\left(\begin{array}[]{c}(x-z)_{1}\,I_{3}\\
(x-z)_{2}\,I_{3}\\
(x-z)_{3}\,I_{3}\end{array}\right).

Based on the estimates given in Proposition
2.5
, and using the notations introduced by Definition
2.6
, we provide in the following proposition the estimates related to the problem
(\ref{U})
’s scattering coefficients.

Proposition 2.7

(Estimation of the scattering coefficients)
.

For
h\in(0,2)
, under assumption (
1.11
), the following estimations hold:

(1)

Regarding the scattering parameter
W_{1}
, defined by (
2.13
), we have

(2.16)

\left\lVert\overset{1}{\mathbb{P}}\left(\tilde{W}_{1}\right)\right\rVert_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(B_{1})}=\mathcal{O}(a^{1-h})\quad\text{and}\quad\left\lVert\overset{j}{\mathbb{P}}\left(\tilde{W}_{1}\right)\right\rVert_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(B_{1})}=\mathcal{O}(a^{3}),\quad\text{for}\quad j=2,3.

(2)

Regarding the scattering parameter
W_{2}
, defined by
(\ref{DefW2})
, we have

(2.17)

\left\lVert\overset{1}{\mathbb{P}}\left(\tilde{W}_{2}\right)\right\rVert_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(B_{2})}=\mathcal{O}(a),\quad\overset{2}{\mathbb{P}}\left(\tilde{W}_{2}\right)\,=\,0\quad\text{and}\quad\left\lVert\overset{3}{\mathbb{P}}\left(\tilde{W}_{2}\right)\right\rVert_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(B_{2})}=\mathcal{O}(a^{5-h}).

(3)

Regarding the scattering parameter
V_{m}
, defined by (
2.15
), for
m=1,2
, we have

(2.18)

\left\lVert\tilde{V}_{1}\right\rVert_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(B_{1})}=\mathcal{O}\left(a^{2}\right)\quad\mbox{and}\quad\left\lVert\tilde{V}_{2}\right\rVert_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(B_{2})}=\mathcal{O}\left(a^{-h}\right).

Proof.

See Subsection
5.3
.
∎

3.
Linear algebraic system of the dimer

In this section, we shall present the linear algebraic system derived from the L.S.E
(\ref{LS eq2})
by projecting the solution
E
onto the two sub-spaces
\mathbb{H}_{0}(\mathrm{div}\,=0)
and
\nabla\mathcal{H}armonic
. We start by using
(\ref{Eq0404})
to derive, for
m=1,2
, the following expression

(3.1)

\overset{1}{\mathbb{P}}(E_{m})\,=\,Curl\left(F_{m}\right),\;\text{in}\;D_{m},\quad\text{with}\quad\nu\times F_{m}\,=\,0,\;\text{on}\;\partial D_{m},\quad\text{and}\quad\mathrm{div}\,(F_{m})\,=\,0,\;\text{in}\;D_{m}.

Set

(3.2)

Q_{m}:=\eta_{m}\int_{D_{m}}F_{m}(y)\,dy\quad\mbox{and}\quad R_{m}:=\eta_{m}\int_{D_{m}}\overset{3}{\mathbb{P}}\left(E_{m}\right)(y)\,dy,

where
\eta_{m}
is defined in (
1.6
).

Proposition 3.1
.

Under
Assumption
1
, for
t,h\in(0,1)
, such that

4\,-\,h\,-\,4\,t\,>\,0,

there holds the coming linear algebraic systems related to the problem
(\ref{U})
,

(3.3)

\begin{pmatrix}I_{3}&0&-\,\mathcal{B}_{13}&-\,\mathcal{B}_{14}\\
0&I_{3}&-\,\mathcal{B}_{23}&-\,\mathcal{B}_{24}\\
-\,\mathcal{B}_{31}&-\,\mathcal{B}_{32}&I_{3}&0\\
-\,\mathcal{B}_{41}&-\,\mathcal{B}_{42}&0&I_{3}\end{pmatrix}\cdot\begin{pmatrix}Q_{1}\\
R_{1}\\
Q_{2}\\
R_{2}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\frac{i\,k\,\eta_{0}}{\pm\,c_{0}}\,a^{3-h}\,{\bf P}_{0,1}^{(1)}\cdot H^{Inc}(z_{1})\\
a^{3}\,{\bf P}_{0,1}^{(2)}\cdot E^{Inc}(z_{1})\\
i\,k\,\eta_{2}\,a^{5}\,{\bf P}_{0,2}^{(1)}\cdot H^{Inc}(z_{2})\\
\frac{\eta_{2}}{\pm\,d_{0}}\,a^{3-h}\,{\bf P}_{0,2}^{(2)}\cdot E^{Inc}(z_{2})\end{pmatrix}+\begin{pmatrix}Error_{1}^{(1)}\\
Error_{1}^{(2)}\\
Error_{2}^{(1)}\\
Error_{2}^{(2)}\end{pmatrix}

with

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{13}\,

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\,\frac{k^{4}\,\eta_{0}}{\pm\,c_{0}}\,a^{3-h}\,{\bf P}_{0,1}^{(1)}\cdot\Upsilon_{k}(z_{1},z_{2})

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{23}\,

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\,k^{2}\,a^{3}\,{\bf P}_{0,1}^{(2)}\cdot\nabla\Phi_{k}(z_{1},z_{2})\times

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{31}\,

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\,k^{4}\,\eta_{2}\,a^{5}\,{\bf P}_{0,2}^{(1)}\cdot\Upsilon_{k}(z_{2},z_{1})

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{41}\,

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\,\frac{k^{2}\,\eta_{2}}{\pm\,d_{0}}\,a^{3-h}\,{\bf P}_{0,2}^{(2)}\cdot\nabla\Phi_{k}(z_{2},z_{1})\times

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{14}\,

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\,\frac{k^{2}\,\eta_{0}}{\pm\,c_{0}}\,a^{3-h}\,{\bf P}_{0,1}^{(1)}\cdot\nabla\Phi_{k}(z_{1},z_{2})\times

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{24}\,

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\,k^{2}\,a^{3}\,{\bf P}_{0,1}^{(2)}\cdot\Upsilon_{k}(z_{1},z_{2})

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{32}\,

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\,k^{2}\,\eta_{2}\,a^{5}\,{\bf P}_{0,2}^{(1)}\cdot\nabla\Phi_{k}(z_{2},z_{1})\times

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{42}\,

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\,\frac{k^{2}\,\eta_{2}}{\pm\,d_{0}}\,a^{3-h}\,{\bf P}_{0,2}^{(2)}\cdot\Upsilon_{k}(z_{2},z_{1})

where

\displaystyle{\bf P}_{0,1}^{(1)}\,

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\,\int_{B_{1}}\phi_{n_{0},B_{1}}(x)\,dx\otimes\int_{B_{1}}\phi_{n_{0},B_{1}}(x)\,dx

\displaystyle{\bf P}_{0,1}^{(2)}\,

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\,\sum_{n}\frac{1}{\lambda_{n}^{(3)}(B_{1})}\int_{B_{1}}e_{n,B_{1}}^{(3)}(x)\,dx\otimes\int_{B_{1}}e_{n,B_{1}}^{(3)}(x)\,dx

\displaystyle{\bf P}_{0,2}^{(1)}\,

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,\sum_{n}\int_{B_{2}}\phi_{n,B_{2}}(x)\,dx\otimes\int_{B_{2}}\phi_{n,B_{2}}(x)\,dx

\displaystyle{\bf P}_{0,2}^{(2)}\,

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,\int_{B_{2}}e_{n_{*},B_{2}}^{(3)}(x)\,dx\otimes\int_{B_{2}}e_{n_{*},B_{2}}^{(3)}(x)\,dx,

with
\phi_{n_{0},B_{1}}(\cdot)
satisfying
(\ref{def-phi-n0})
and
\phi_{n,B_{2}}(\cdot)
is given by
(\ref{pre-cond})
. Besides,

\displaystyle Error_{1}^{(1)}=\,\mathcal{O}\left(a^{\min(3;7-2h-3t)}\right),

\displaystyle Error_{1}^{(2)}=\,\mathcal{O}\left(a^{\min(4;7-h-4t)}\right),

\displaystyle Error_{2}^{(1)}=\mathcal{O}(a^{\min(6;9-h-4t)}),

\displaystyle Error_{2}^{(2)}=\mathcal{O}\left(a^{\min(4-h;7-h-4t;6-3t;7-2h-3t)}\right).

Proof.

See Section
6
.
∎

4.
Proof of the main result

In this section, the proof of Theorem
1.1
is presented as the following four steps with all the necessary propositions given in the previous sections.

(I). Derivation of the scattered wave
E^{s}(x)
.

Thanks to the L.S.E given by
(\ref{LS eq2})
and the fact that
E\,=\,E^{Inc}\,+\,E^{s}
, we deduce that

E^{s}(x)\,=\,-\,\nabla M^{k}_{D}(\eta\,E)(x)\,+\,k^{2}\,N^{k}_{D}(\eta\,E)(x),

which, by letting
x
outside
D
and using
(\ref{dyadicG})
, can be rewritten as

E^{s}(x)\,=\,k^{2}\,\int_{D}\Upsilon_{k}(x,y)\cdot\eta(y)\,E(y)\,dy\,=\,k^{2}\,\sum_{m=1}^{2}\eta_{m}\,\int_{D_{m}}\Upsilon_{k}(x,y)\cdot E_{m}(y)\,dy.

Besides, by splitting
E_{m}
as
E_{m}=\overset{1}{\mathbb{P}}\left(E_{m}\right)\,+\,\overset{3}{\mathbb{P}}\left(E_{m}\right)
, see
(\ref{Equa1108})
, and using the fact that
\nabla M^{k}(\cdot)|_{\mathbb{H}_{0}\left(\mathrm{div}\,=0\right)}
is a vanishing operator we get

\displaystyle E^{s}(x)\,

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,k^{2}\,\sum_{m=1}^{2}\eta_{m}\,\int_{D_{m}}\Phi_{k}(x,y)\,\overset{1}{\mathbb{P}}\left(E_{m}\right)(y)\,dy+k^{2}\,\sum_{m=1}^{2}\eta_{m}\,\int_{D_{m}}\Upsilon_{k}(x,y)\cdot\overset{3}{\mathbb{P}}\left(E_{m}\right)(y)\,dy

\displaystyle\overset{(\ref{def-F_j})}{=}

\displaystyle\,-\,k^{2}\,\sum_{m=1}^{2}\eta_{m}\,\int_{D_{m}}\underset{y}{\nabla}\Phi_{k}(x,y)\times F_{m}(y)\,dy+k^{2}\,\sum_{m=1}^{2}\eta_{m}\,\int_{D_{m}}\Upsilon_{k}(x,y)\cdot\overset{3}{\mathbb{P}}\left(E_{m}\right)(y)\,dy.

On the R.H.S of the above expression, by expanding
\nabla\Phi_{k}(x,\cdot)
and
\Upsilon_{k}(x,\cdot)
, near the center
z_{m}
, with
m=1,2
, and using
(\ref{Qj1})
we obtain

(4.1)

E^{s}(x)\,=\,-\,k^{2}\,\sum_{m=1}^{2}\,\left[\underset{y}{\nabla}\Phi_{k}(x,z_{m})\times Q_{m}\,-\,\Upsilon_{k}(x,z_{m})\cdot R_{m}\right]\,+\,Remainder,

where
Remainder
is given by

\displaystyle Remainder\,

\displaystyle:=

\displaystyle\,\,-\,k^{2}\,\sum_{m=1}^{2}\eta_{m}\,\int_{D_{m}}\int_{0}^{1}\underset{y}{\nabla}\underset{y}{\nabla}\Phi_{k}(x,z_{m}+t(y-z_{m}))\cdot(y-z_{m})\,dt\times F_{m}(y)\,dy

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle k^{2}\,\sum_{m=1}^{2}\eta_{m}\,\int_{D_{m}}\int_{0}^{1}\underset{y}{\nabla}\Upsilon_{k}(x,z_{m}+t(y-z_{m}))\cdot\mathcal{P}\left(y,z_{m}\right)dt\cdot\overset{3}{\mathbb{P}}\left(E_{m}\right)(y)\,dy.

Next, we estimate the term
Remainder
.

\displaystyle\left|Remainder\right|\,

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\,\,\sum_{m=1}^{2}\left|\eta_{m}\right|\,\left\|\int_{0}^{1}\underset{y}{\nabla}\underset{y}{\nabla}\Phi_{k}(x,z_{m}+t(\cdot-z_{m}))\cdot(\cdot-z_{m})\,dt\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(D_{m})}\;\left\|F_{m}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(D_{m})}

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\,\sum_{m=1}^{2}\left|\eta_{m}\right|\,\left\|\int_{0}^{1}\underset{y}{\nabla}\Upsilon_{k}(x,z_{m}+t(\cdot-z_{m}))\cdot\mathcal{P}\left(\cdot,z_{m}\right)dt\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(D_{m})}\,\left\|\overset{3}{\mathbb{P}}\left(E_{m}\right)\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(D_{m})}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\,a^{4}\,\sum_{m=1}^{2}\left|\eta_{m}\right|\,\left[\left\|\tilde{F}_{m}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(B_{m})}\,+\,\left\|\overset{3}{\mathbb{P}}\left(\tilde{E}_{m}\right)\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(B_{m})}\right].

Thanks to
(\ref{max-P1P3}),(\ref{def-eta})
, and the formula
(\ref{F2aP1E2})
, we deduce that

Remainder\,=\,\mathcal{O}\left(a^{4-h}\right).

Hence,
(\ref{SMEq0758})
becomes,

(4.2)

E^{s}(x)\,=\,-\,k^{2}\,\sum_{m=1}^{2}\,\left[\underset{y}{\nabla}\Phi_{k}(x,z_{m})\times Q_{m}\,-\,\Upsilon_{k}(x,z_{m})\cdot R_{m}\right]\,+\,\mathcal{O}\left(a^{4-h}\right),

where
\left(Q_{1},R_{1},Q_{2},R_{2}\right)
is solution of
(\ref{al-D1})
.

(II). Derivation of the far-field
E^{\infty}(\hat{x})
.

To estimate the far-field, we use the fact that
E\,=\,E^{Inc}\,+\,E^{s}
, the formula
(\ref{N-M opera})

and formula
(\ref{def-far})
, to obtain

\displaystyle E^{\infty}(\hat{x})\,

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,\frac{k^{2}}{4\,\pi}\,\left(I-\hat{x}\otimes\hat{x}\right)\cdot\int_{D}e^{-\,i\,k\,\hat{x}\cdot y}\;\eta(y)\;E(y)\;dy,\quad D=D_{1}\cup D_{2}.

\displaystyle\overset{(\ref{def-eta})}{=}

\displaystyle\,\frac{k^{2}}{4\,\pi}\,\eta_{1}\,\left(I-\hat{x}\otimes\hat{x}\right)\cdot\int_{D_{1}}e^{-\,i\,k\,\hat{x}\cdot y}\;E_{1}(y)\;dy\;+\;\,\frac{k^{2}}{4\,\pi}\,\eta_{2}\,\left(I-\hat{x}\otimes\hat{x}\right)\cdot\int_{D_{2}}e^{-i\,k\,\hat{x}\cdot y}\;E_{2}(y)\;dy.

By expanding the function
y\rightarrow e^{-\,i\,k\,\hat{x}\cdot y}
at
y=z_{m}
, with
m=1,2
, we obtain

(4.3)

E^{\infty}(\hat{x})\,=\,\frac{k^{2}}{4\,\pi}\,\sum_{m=1}^{2}\,\eta_{m}\,\left(I-\hat{x}\otimes\hat{x}\right)\cdot\int_{D_{m}}\left[e^{-ik\hat{x}\cdot z_{m}}-ike^{-ik\hat{x}\cdot z_{m}}(y-z_{m})\cdot\hat{x}\right]E_{m}(y)dy\,+\,\sum_{m=1}^{2}R^{(1)}_{m},

where

R^{(1)}_{m}\,:=\,\frac{k^{4}}{8\,\pi}\,\eta_{m}\,(I-\hat{x}\otimes\hat{x})\cdot\int_{D_{m}}\left((y-z_{m})\cdot\hat{x}\right)^{2}\int_{0}^{1}(1-t)e^{-ik\hat{x}\cdot(z_{1}+t(y-z_{m}))}\,dtE_{m}(y)\,dy,\quad m=1,2.

It is direct to get from
(\ref{def-eta})
that, there holds

(4.4)

R_{1}^{(1)}\,=\,\mathcal{O}\left(a^{\frac{3}{2}}\left\lVert E_{1}\right\rVert_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(D_{1})}\right)\quad\text{and}\quad R_{2}^{(1)}\,=\,\mathcal{O}\left(a^{\frac{7}{2}}\left\lVert E_{2}\right\rVert_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(D_{2})}\right).

Based on Proposition
2.4
, formula
(\ref{*add1})
, by splitting
E_{m}
as
E_{m}=\overset{1}{\mathbb{P}}\left(E_{m}\right)\,+\,\overset{3}{\mathbb{P}}\left(E_{m}\right)
, we use

(\ref{RefNeeded1})
, and the estimations derived in
(\ref{es-R11})
, to rewrite
(\ref{eq-far2})
as,

(4.5)

\displaystyle E^{\infty}(\hat{x})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{k^{2}}{4\,\pi}\,\sum_{m=1}^{2}\,\eta_{m}\,\left(I-\hat{x}\otimes\hat{x}\right)\cdot e^{-ik\hat{x}\cdot z_{m}}\,\int_{D_{m}}\overset{3}{\mathbb{P}}\left(E_{m}\right)(y)dy

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle i\,\frac{k^{3}}{4\,\pi}\,\sum_{m=1}^{2}\eta_{m}\left(I-\hat{x}\otimes\hat{x}\right)\cdot e^{-ik\hat{x}\cdot z_{m}}\,\int_{D_{m}}\hat{x}\cdot(y-z_{m})\;\overset{1}{\mathbb{P}}\left(E_{m}\right)(y)dy

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle R_{1}^{(2)}\,+\,R_{2}^{(2)}\,+\,\mathcal{O}\left(a^{\frac{3}{2}}\left\lVert E_{1}\right\rVert_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(D_{1})}\right)+\mathcal{O}\,\left(a^{\frac{7}{2}}\left\lVert E_{2}\right\rVert_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(D_{2})}\right),

where

R_{m}^{(2)}\;:=\;-i\,\frac{k^{3}}{4\pi}\,\eta_{m}\left(I\,-\,\hat{x}\otimes\hat{x}\right)e^{-ik\hat{x}\cdot z_{m}}\cdot\int_{D_{m}}\hat{x}\cdot(y-z_{m})\overset{3}{\mathbb{P}}(E_{m})(y)\,dy,\quad m=1,2.

Then, using
(\ref{def-eta})
, the following estimation holds,

R_{1}^{(2)}\,=\,\mathcal{O}\left(a^{\frac{1}{2}}\,\left\lVert\overset{3}{\mathbb{P}}(E_{1})\right\rVert_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(D_{1})}\right)\quad\text{and}\quad R_{2}^{(2)}\,=\,\mathcal{O}\left(a^{\frac{5}{2}}\,\left\lVert\overset{3}{\mathbb{P}}(E_{2})\right\rVert_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(D_{2})}\right).

Thus, we can deduce from (
4.5
) that,

\displaystyle E^{\infty}(\hat{x})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{k^{2}}{4\,\pi}\,\sum_{m=1}^{2}\,\eta_{m}\,\left(I-\hat{x}\otimes\hat{x}\right)\cdot e^{-ik\hat{x}\cdot z_{m}}\,\int_{D_{m}}\overset{3}{\mathbb{P}}\left(E_{m}\right)(y)dy

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle i\,\frac{k^{3}}{4\,\pi}\,\,\sum_{m=1}^{2}\eta_{m}\left(I-\hat{x}\otimes\hat{x}\right)\cdot e^{-ik\hat{x}\cdot z_{m}}\,\int_{D_{m}}\hat{x}\cdot(y-z_{m})\;\overset{1}{\mathbb{P}}\left(E_{m}\right)(y)dy

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}\left(a^{\frac{1}{2}}\,\left\lVert\overset{3}{\mathbb{P}}(E_{1})\right\rVert_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(D_{1})}\right)\,+\,\mathcal{O}\left(a^{\frac{5}{2}}\,\left\lVert\overset{3}{\mathbb{P}}(E_{2})\right\rVert_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(D_{2})}\right)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\,\mathcal{O}\left(a^{\frac{3}{2}}\left\lVert E_{1}\right\rVert_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(D_{1})}\right)+\,\mathcal{O}\,\left(a^{\frac{7}{2}}\left\lVert E_{2}\right\rVert_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(D_{2})}\right),

which, by using (
3.1
) and the notations given by (
3.2
), indicates

(4.6)

E^{\infty}(\hat{x})\,=\,\frac{k^{2}}{4\,\pi}\,\sum_{m=1}^{2}\,e^{-\,ik\hat{x}\cdot z_{m}}\,\left[\left(I-\hat{x}\otimes\hat{x}\right)\cdot R_{m}\,+\,i\,k\,\,\hat{x}\times Q_{m}\right]\,+\,Error^{(1)},

where the vectors
\left(Q_{m},R_{m}\right)_{m=1,2}
are the solutions of the linear algebraic system given by (
1.15
), and

\resizebox{31382734}{}{$Error^{(1)}\,:=\,\mathcal{O}\left(\,a^{\frac{1}{2}}\,\left\lVert\overset{3}{\mathbb{P}}(E_{1})\right\rVert_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(D_{1})}\right)\,+\,\mathcal{O}\left(\,a^{\frac{5}{2}}\,\left\lVert\overset{3}{\mathbb{P}}(E_{2})\right\rVert_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(D_{2})}\right)+\,\mathcal{O}\left(a^{\frac{3}{2}}\left\lVert E_{1}\right\rVert_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(D_{1})}\right)+\,\mathcal{O}\,\left(a^{\frac{7}{2}}\left\lVert E_{2}\right\rVert_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(D_{2})}\right)$},

which can be estimated, by using Proposition
2.4
and Proposition
2.5
, as
\mathcal{O}\left(a^{4-h}\right)
. Hence,
(\ref{IA1})
becomes,

(4.7)

E^{\infty}(\hat{x})\,=\,\frac{k^{2}}{4\,\pi}\,\sum_{m=1}^{2}\,e^{-\,ik\hat{x}\cdot z_{m}}\,\left[\left(I-\hat{x}\otimes\hat{x}\right)\cdot R_{m}\,+\,i\,k\,\,\hat{x}\times Q_{m}\right]\,+\,\mathcal{O}\left(a^{4-h}\right),

where
\left(Q_{1},R_{1},Q_{2},R_{2}\right)
is solution of
(\ref{al-D1})
.

We have seen from the estimation of the scattered field
E^{s}(\cdot)
, given by
(\ref{Eq1132})
, and the estimation of the far field
E^{\infty}(\cdot)
, given by
(\ref{Eq0254})
, that the determination of
\left(Q_{1},R_{1},Q_{2},R_{2}\right)
is of great importance to achieve the estimation of both
E^{s}(\cdot)
and
E^{\infty}(\cdot)
.
The goal of the next step is to determine the dominant part related to
\left(Q_{1},R_{1},Q_{2},R_{2}\right)
.

(III). Invertibility of the algebraic system (
3.3
).

We recall that
\left(Q_{1},R_{1},Q_{2},R_{2}\right)
is the solution to

(4.8)

\begin{pmatrix}I_{3}&0&-\,\mathcal{B}_{13}&-\,\mathcal{B}_{14}\\
0&I_{3}&-\,\mathcal{B}_{23}&-\,\mathcal{B}_{24}\\
-\,\mathcal{B}_{31}&-\,\mathcal{B}_{32}&I_{3}&0\\
-\,\mathcal{B}_{41}&-\,\mathcal{B}_{42}&0&I_{3}\end{pmatrix}\cdot\begin{pmatrix}Q_{1}\\
R_{1}\\
Q_{2}\\
R_{2}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\frac{i\,k\,\eta_{0}}{\pm\,c_{0}}\,a^{3-h}\,{\bf P}_{0,1}^{(1)}\cdot H^{Inc}(z_{1})\\
a^{3}\,{\bf P}_{0,1}^{(2)}\cdot E^{Inc}(z_{1})\\
i\,k\,\eta_{2}\,a^{5}\,{\bf P}_{0,2}^{(1)}\cdot H^{Inc}(z_{2})\\
\frac{\eta_{2}}{\pm\,d_{0}}\,a^{3-h}\,{\bf P}_{0,2}^{(2)}\cdot E^{Inc}(z_{2})\end{pmatrix}\,+\,\begin{pmatrix}Error_{1}^{(1)}\\
Error_{1}^{(2)}\\
Error_{2}^{(1)}\\
Error_{2}^{(2)}\end{pmatrix}.

Notice that the R.H.S of
(\ref{PerAS})
is perturbed by the presence of an additive vector error term.
Next, we investigate the associated unperturbed algebraic system given by

(4.9)

\begin{pmatrix}I_{3}&0&-\,\mathcal{B}_{13}&-\,\mathcal{B}_{14}\\
0&I_{3}&-\,\mathcal{B}_{23}&-\,\mathcal{B}_{24}\\
-\,\mathcal{B}_{31}&-\,\mathcal{B}_{32}&I_{3}&0\\
-\,\mathcal{B}_{41}&-\,\mathcal{B}_{42}&0&I_{3}\end{pmatrix}\cdot\begin{pmatrix}\tilde{Q}_{1}\\
\tilde{R}_{1}\\
\tilde{Q}_{2}\\
\tilde{R}_{2}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\frac{i\,k\,\eta_{0}}{\pm\,c_{0}}\,a^{3-h}\,{\bf P}_{0,1}^{(1)}\cdot H^{Inc}(z_{1})\\
a^{3}\,{\bf P}_{0,1}^{(2)}\cdot E^{Inc}(z_{1})\\
i\,k\,\eta_{2}\,a^{5}\,{\bf P}_{0,2}^{(1)}\cdot H^{Inc}(z_{2})\\
\frac{\eta_{2}}{\pm\,d_{0}}\,a^{3-h}\,{\bf P}_{0,2}^{(2)}\cdot E^{Inc}(z_{2})\end{pmatrix}.

It is clear that the difference between the solution to
(\ref{unPerAS})
and the solution to
(\ref{PerAS})
satisfies the following algebraic system,

\begin{pmatrix}I_{3}&0&-\,\mathcal{B}_{13}&-\,\mathcal{B}_{14}\\
0&I_{3}&-\,\mathcal{B}_{23}&-\,\mathcal{B}_{24}\\
-\,\mathcal{B}_{31}&-\,\mathcal{B}_{32}&I_{3}&0\\
-\,\mathcal{B}_{41}&-\,\mathcal{B}_{42}&0&I_{3}\end{pmatrix}\cdot\begin{pmatrix}Q_{1}\,-\,\tilde{Q}_{1}\\
R_{1}\,-\,\tilde{R}_{1}\\
Q_{2}\,-\,\tilde{Q}_{2}\\
R_{2}\,-\,\tilde{R}_{2}\end{pmatrix}\,=\,\begin{pmatrix}Error_{1}^{(1)}\\
Error_{1}^{(2)}\\
Error_{2}^{(1)}\\
Error_{2}^{(2)}\end{pmatrix},

which is invertible under the condition
4\,-\,h\,-\,4\,t\,>\,0
. By inverting the above system using Born series, we obtain

(4.10)

\begin{pmatrix}Q_{1}\,-\,\tilde{Q}_{1}\\
R_{1}\,-\,\tilde{R}_{1}\\
Q_{2}\,-\,\tilde{Q}_{2}\\
R_{2}\,-\,\tilde{R}_{2}\end{pmatrix}\,=\,\sum_{n\geq 0}\mathbb{K}_{n}\cdot\begin{pmatrix}Error_{1}^{(1)}\\
Error_{1}^{(2)}\\
Error_{2}^{(1)}\\
Error_{2}^{(2)}\end{pmatrix},

where the matrix
\mathbb{K}_{n}
is given by

(4.11)

\mathbb{K}_{n}\,:=\,\begin{pmatrix}0&0&\mathcal{B}_{13}&\mathcal{B}_{14}\\
0&0&\mathcal{B}_{23}&\mathcal{B}_{24}\\
\mathcal{B}_{31}&\mathcal{B}_{32}&0&0\\
\mathcal{B}_{41}&\mathcal{B}_{42}&0&0\end{pmatrix}^{n}.

In order to evaluate the L.H.S of
(\ref{QRKErr})
, we keep only the zeroth order term and the first order term on the R.H.S of the Born series to derive the following estimations

\displaystyle|Q_{1}-\tilde{Q}_{1}|\,

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\,Error_{1}^{(1)}\,+\,a^{3-h}\,d^{-3}\,Error_{2}^{(1)}\,+\,a^{3-h}\,d^{-2}\,Error_{2}^{(2)}\;=\;\mathcal{O}\left(a^{\min(3;7-2h-3t)}\right),

\displaystyle|R_{1}-\tilde{R}_{1}|

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\,Error_{1}^{(2)}\,+\,\left(a^{3}\,d^{-2}\,+\,a^{9-2h}\,d^{-8}\right)\,Error_{2}^{(1)}\,+\,a^{3}\,d^{-3}Error_{2}^{(2)}\;=\;\mathcal{O}\left(a^{\min(4;7-h-4t)}\right),

\displaystyle|Q_{2}-\tilde{Q}_{2}|

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle Error_{2}^{(1)}\,+\,a^{5}\,d^{-3}\,Error_{1}^{(1)}\,+\,\left(a^{5}d^{-2}+a^{11-2h}d^{-8}\right)\,Error_{1}^{(2)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\mathcal{O}\left(a^{\min(6;9-h-4t;8-3t)}\right),

(4.12)

\displaystyle|R_{2}-\tilde{R}_{2}|\,

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\,Error_{2}^{(2)}\,+\,a^{3-h}\,d^{-2}\,Error_{1}^{(1)}\,+\,a^{3-h}\,d^{-3}\,Error_{1}^{(2)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\mathcal{O}\left(a^{\min(4-h;7-h-4t;6-3t;7-2h-3t;10-2h-7t)}\right).

(IV). The corresponding revised Foldy-Lax approximation.

We recall first the scattered field expansion given by
(\ref{Eq1132})
,

E^{s}(x)\,=\,-\,k^{2}\,\sum_{m=1}^{2}\,\left[\underset{y}{\nabla}\Phi_{k}(x,z_{m})\times Q_{m}\,-\,\Upsilon_{k}(x,z_{m})\cdot R_{m}\right]\,+\,\mathcal{O}\left(a^{4-h}\right),

where
\left(Q_{1},R_{1},Q_{2},R_{2}\right)
is solution to
(\ref{al-D1})
. Now, by using the estimates derived in
(\ref{es-error-correction})
, under the condition
4-h-4t>0
, and the fact that
x
is away from
D
, we obtain

E^{s}(x)\,=\,-\,k^{2}\,\sum_{m=1}^{2}\,\left[\underset{y}{\nabla}\Phi_{k}(x,z_{m})\times\tilde{Q}_{m}\,-\,\Upsilon_{k}(x,z_{m})\cdot\tilde{R}_{m}\right]\,+\,\mathcal{O}\left(a^{\min(3;\,7-2h-3t;\,10-2h-7t)}\right),

where
\left(\tilde{Q}_{1},\tilde{R}_{1},\tilde{Q}_{2},\tilde{R}_{2}\right)
is solution to the unperturbed algebraic system
(\ref{unPerAS})
.
This justify
(\ref{general-scattered-field})
. Similarly, by recalling the far-field expression given by
(\ref{Eq0254})
and using the estimates derived in
(\ref{es-error-correction})
, with the condition
4-h-4t>0
, and the fact that
x
is away from
D
, we can obtain

E^{\infty}(\hat{x})=\frac{k^{2}}{4\pi}\sum_{m=1}^{2}\,e^{-ik\hat{x}\cdot z_{m}}\left[(I-\hat{x}\otimes\hat{x})\tilde{R}_{m}\,+\,i\,k\,\hat{x}\,\otimes\,\tilde{Q}_{m}\right]\,+\,\mathcal{O}(a^{\min(3;7-2h-3t;10-2h-7t)}),

where
\left(\tilde{Q}_{1},\tilde{R}_{1},\tilde{Q}_{2},\tilde{R}_{2}\right)
is solution to
(\ref{unPerAS})
. This justify
(\ref{approximation-E})
, and ends the proof of Theorem
1.1
.

4.1.
Proof of Corollary
1.2

We start by recalling, from
(\ref{unPerAS})
, that
\left(\tilde{Q}_{1},\tilde{R}_{1},\tilde{Q}_{2},\tilde{R}_{2}\right)
is a vector solution of the following algebraic system

\begin{pmatrix}I_{3}&0&-\,\mathcal{B}_{13}&-\,\mathcal{B}_{14}\\
0&I_{3}&-\,\mathcal{B}_{23}&-\,\mathcal{B}_{24}\\
-\,\mathcal{B}_{31}&-\,\mathcal{B}_{32}&I_{3}&0\\
-\,\mathcal{B}_{41}&-\,\mathcal{B}_{42}&0&I_{3}\end{pmatrix}\cdot\begin{pmatrix}\tilde{Q}_{1}\\
\tilde{R}_{1}\\
\tilde{Q}_{2}\\
\tilde{R}_{2}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\frac{i\,k\,\eta_{0}}{\pm\,c_{0}}\,a^{3-h}\,{\bf P}_{0,1}^{(1)}\cdot H^{Inc}(z_{1})\\
a^{3}\,{\bf P}_{0,1}^{(2)}\cdot E^{Inc}(z_{1})\\
i\,k\,\eta_{2}\,a^{5}\,{\bf P}_{0,2}^{(1)}\cdot H^{Inc}(z_{2})\\
\frac{\eta_{2}}{\pm\,d_{0}}\,a^{3-h}\,{\bf P}_{0,2}^{(2)}\cdot E^{Inc}(z_{2})\end{pmatrix},

which is invertible under the condition
4\,-\,h\,-\,4\,t\,>\,0
. By inverting the above system using Born series, we obtain

(4.13)

\begin{pmatrix}\tilde{Q}_{1}\\
\tilde{R}_{1}\\
\tilde{Q}_{2}\\
\tilde{R}_{2}\end{pmatrix}\,=\,\begin{pmatrix}\frac{i\,k\,\eta_{0}}{\pm\,c_{0}}\,a^{3-h}\,{\bf P}_{0,1}^{(1)}\cdot H^{Inc}(z_{1})\\
a^{3}\,{\bf P}_{0,1}^{(2)}\cdot E^{Inc}(z_{1})\\
i\,k\,\eta_{2}\,a^{5}\,{\bf P}_{0,2}^{(1)}\cdot H^{Inc}(z_{2})\\
\frac{\eta_{2}}{\pm\,d_{0}}\,a^{3-h}\,{\bf P}_{0,2}^{(2)}\cdot E^{Inc}(z_{2})\end{pmatrix}\,+\,\sum_{n\geq 1}\mathbb{K}_{n}\cdot\begin{pmatrix}\frac{i\,k\,\eta_{0}}{\pm\,c_{0}}\,a^{3-h}\,{\bf P}_{0,1}^{(1)}\cdot H^{Inc}(z_{1})\\
a^{3}\,{\bf P}_{0,1}^{(2)}\cdot E^{Inc}(z_{1})\\
i\,k\,\eta_{2}\,a^{5}\,{\bf P}_{0,2}^{(1)}\cdot H^{Inc}(z_{2})\\
\frac{\eta_{2}}{\pm\,d_{0}}\,a^{3-h}\,{\bf P}_{0,2}^{(2)}\cdot E^{Inc}(z_{2})\end{pmatrix},

where the matrix
\mathbb{K}_{n}
is given by
(\ref{DefKn})
.
By keeping the dominant part of
\sum_{n\geq 1}\mathbb{K}_{n}
, which is
\mathbb{K}_{1}
, the second term on the R.H.S of the above equation will be reduced to

\mathbb{K}_{1}\cdot\begin{pmatrix}\frac{i\,k\,\eta_{0}}{\pm\,c_{0}}\,a^{3-h}\,{\bf P}_{0,1}^{(1)}\cdot H^{Inc}(z_{1})\\
a^{3}\,{\bf P}_{0,1}^{(2)}\cdot E^{Inc}(z_{1})\\
i\,k\,\eta_{2}\,a^{5}\,{\bf P}_{0,2}^{(1)}\cdot H^{Inc}(z_{2})\\
\frac{\eta_{2}}{\pm\,d_{0}}\,a^{3-h}\,{\bf P}_{0,2}^{(2)}\cdot E^{Inc}(z_{2})\end{pmatrix}\,\simeq\begin{pmatrix}i\,k\,\eta_{2}\,a^{5}\,\mathcal{B}_{13}\cdot{\bf P}_{0,2}^{(1)}\cdot H^{Inc}(z_{2})\,+\,\frac{\eta_{2}}{\pm\,d_{0}}\,a^{3-h}\,\mathcal{B}_{23}\cdot{\bf P}_{0,2}^{(2)}\cdot E^{Inc}(z_{2})\\
i\,k\,\eta_{2}\,a^{5}\,\mathcal{B}_{23}\cdot{\bf P}_{0,2}^{(1)}\cdot H^{Inc}(z_{2})\,+\,\frac{\eta_{2}}{\pm\,d_{0}}\,a^{3-h}\,\mathcal{B}_{24}\cdot{\bf P}_{0,2}^{(2)}\cdot E^{Inc}(z_{2})\\
\frac{i\,k\,\eta_{0}}{\pm\,c_{0}}\,a^{3-h}\,\mathcal{B}_{31}\cdot{\bf P}_{0,1}^{(1)}\cdot H^{Inc}(z_{1})\,+\,a^{3}\,\mathcal{B}_{32}\cdot{\bf P}_{0,1}^{(2)}\cdot E^{Inc}(z_{1})\\
\frac{i\,k\,\eta_{0}}{\pm\,c_{0}}\,a^{3-h}\,\mathcal{B}_{41}\cdot{\bf P}_{0,1}^{(1)}\cdot H^{Inc}(z_{1})\,+\,a^{3}\,\mathcal{B}_{42}\cdot{\bf P}_{0,1}^{(2)}\cdot E^{Inc}(z_{1})\end{pmatrix}.

By estimating component by component the above vector, using the expression of the matrix
\mathcal{B}_{ij}
, given in Proposition
3.1
, and plugging the obtained result into
(\ref{Sol=S+Err})
we can obtain the following estimations

\displaystyle\tilde{Q}_{1}\,

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,\frac{i\,\eta_{0}\,k}{\pm\,c_{0}}\,a^{3-h}\,{\bf P}_{0,1}^{(1)}\cdot H_{1}^{Inc}(z_{1})\,+\,\mathcal{O}\left(a^{9-3h-5t}\right),

\displaystyle\tilde{R}_{1}\,

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,a^{3}\,{\bf P}_{0,1}^{(2)}\cdot E_{1}^{Inc}(z_{1})\,+\,\frac{k^{2}\eta_{0}}{\pm d_{0}}\,a^{6-h}\,{\bf P}_{0,1}^{(2)}\cdot\Upsilon_{k}(z_{1},z_{2})\cdot{\bf P}_{0,2}^{(2)}\cdot E^{Inc}(z_{2})\,+\,\mathcal{O}(a^{9-2h-5t}),

\displaystyle\tilde{Q}_{2}\,

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,i\,\eta_{2}\,k\,a^{5}\,{\bf P}_{0,2}^{(1)}\cdot H_{2}^{Inc}(z_{2})\,+\,\frac{ik^{5}\eta_{0}\eta_{2}}{\pm c_{0}}\,a^{8-h}\,{\bf P}_{0,2}^{(1)}\cdot\Upsilon_{k}(z_{2},z_{1})\cdot{\bf P}_{0,1}^{(1)}\cdot H^{Inc}(z_{1})\,+\,\mathcal{O}(a^{11-2h}d^{-5}),

\displaystyle\tilde{R}_{2}\,

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,\frac{\eta_{2}}{\pm\,d_{0}}\,a^{3-h}\,{\bf P}_{0,2}^{(2)}\cdot E_{2}^{Inc}(z_{2})\,+\,\mathcal{O}\left(a^{\min(11-2h-5t;14-3h-8t)}\right).

It is direct to observe from the above estimations that
\left(\tilde{Q}_{1},\tilde{R}_{2}\right)
dominates
\left(\tilde{R}_{1},\tilde{Q}_{2}\right)
. Consequently, by returning back to (
1.13
) and using the above observation we can get that

\displaystyle E^{s}(x)\,

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,k^{2}\,\left[\Upsilon_{k}(x,z_{2})\cdot\tilde{R}_{2}\,-\,\underset{y}{\nabla}\Phi_{k}(x,z_{1})\times\tilde{Q}_{1}\right]

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\,k^{2}\,\left[\Upsilon_{k}(x,z_{1})\cdot\tilde{R}_{1}\,-\,\underset{y}{\nabla}\Phi_{k}(x,z_{2})\times\tilde{Q}_{2}\right]+\,\mathcal{O}\left(a^{\min(3;7-2h-3t;10-2h-7t)}\right).

Since
x
is away from
D
for the scattered wave, by utilizing the estimation of
\left(\tilde{R}_{1},\tilde{Q}_{2}\right)
, we can know that the second term on the R.H.S is estimated of order
\mathcal{O}\left(a^{3}\right)
. Hence, with (
4.1
),

\displaystyle E^{s}(x)\,

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,k^{2}\,\left[\Upsilon_{k}(x,z_{2})\cdot\tilde{R}_{2}\,-\,\underset{y}{\nabla}\Phi_{k}(x,z_{1})\times\tilde{Q}_{1}\right]\,+\,\mathcal{O}\left(a^{\min(3;7-2h-3t;10-2h-7t)}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,\pm\,k^{2}\,a^{3-h}\,\left[\frac{\eta_{2}}{d_{0}}\,\Upsilon_{k}(x,z_{2})\cdot{\bf P}_{0,2}^{(2)}\cdot E_{2}^{Inc}(z_{2})\,-\,\frac{i\,k\,\eta_{0}}{c_{0}}\,\underset{y}{\nabla}\Phi_{k}(x,z_{1})\times{\bf P}_{0,1}^{(1)}\cdot H_{1}^{Inc}(z_{1})\right]

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\,\mathcal{O}\left(a^{\min(3;7-2h-3t;10-2h-7t;9-3h-5t)}\right),

which, by taking Taylor expansion of
H^{Inc}(\cdot)
and
E^{Inc}(\cdot)
at the intermediate point, between
z_{1}
and
z_{2}
with
\left|z_{1}\,-\,z_{2}\right|\,\lesssim\,a^{t}
, i.e.,

\displaystyle H_{1}^{Inc}(z_{1})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle H^{Inc}(z_{0})+ik\int_{0}^{1}e^{ik\theta\cdot(z_{0}+t(z_{1}-z_{0}))}(\theta^{\perp}\times\theta)\left\langle\theta,(z_{1}-z_{0})\right\rangle\,dt\,=\,H^{Inc}(z_{0})\,+\,\mathcal{O}\left(a^{t}\right),

\displaystyle E_{2}^{Inc}(z_{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle E^{Inc}(z_{0})+ik\int_{0}^{1}e^{ik\theta\cdot(z_{0}+t(z_{2}-z_{0}))}(\theta^{\perp}\times\theta)\left\langle\theta,(z_{2}-z_{0})\right\rangle\,dt\,=\,E^{Inc}(z_{0})\,+\,\mathcal{O}\left(a^{t}\right),

gives us under the condition
4-h-4t>0
that,

(4.15)

\displaystyle E^{s}(x)\,

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,\pm\,k^{2}\,a^{3-h}\,\left[\frac{\eta_{2}}{d_{0}}\,\Upsilon_{k}(x,z_{2})\cdot{\bf P}_{0,2}^{(2)}\cdot E^{Inc}(z_{0})\,-\,\frac{i\,k\,\eta_{0}}{c_{0}}\,\underset{y}{\nabla}\Phi_{k}(x,z_{1})\times{\bf P}_{0,1}^{(1)}\cdot H^{Inc}(z_{0})\right]

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\,\mathcal{O}\left(a^{\min(3-h+t;\;3;\;10-2h-7t;\;9-3h-5t)}\right).

Based on (
4.15
), the last step consists in taking the Taylor expansion near the intermediate point
z_{0}
for the functions
\Upsilon_{k}(x,\cdot)
and
\nabla\Phi_{k}(x,\cdot)
, i.e.,

\Upsilon_{k}(x,z_{2})\,=\,\Upsilon_{k}(x,z_{0})\,+\,\mathcal{O}\left(a^{t}\right)\quad\text{and}\quad\nabla\Phi_{k}(x,z_{1})\,=\,\nabla\Phi_{k}(x,z_{0})\,+\,\mathcal{O}\left(a^{t}\right),

where we can use the fact that
x
is away from
D
to derive,

\displaystyle E^{s}(x)\,

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,\pm\,k^{2}\,a^{3-h}\,\left[\frac{\eta_{2}}{d_{0}}\,\Upsilon_{k}(x,z_{0})\cdot{\bf P}_{0,2}^{(2)}\cdot E^{Inc}(z_{0})\,-\,\frac{i\,k\,\eta_{0}}{c_{0}}\,\underset{y}{\nabla}\Phi_{k}(x,z_{0})\times{\bf P}_{0,1}^{(1)}\cdot H^{Inc}(z_{0})\right]

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\,\mathcal{O}\left(a^{\min(3-h+t;\;3;\;10-2h-7t;\;9-3h-5t)}\right),

which justifies
(\ref{CoroSF})
. Furthermore, by returning back to (
1.14
) we obtain

\displaystyle E^{\infty}(\hat{x})\,

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,\frac{k^{2}}{4\,\pi}\left[\,e^{-\,ik\hat{x}\cdot z_{2}}\,\left(I-\hat{x}\otimes\hat{x}\right)\cdot\tilde{R}_{2}\,+\,i\,k\,e^{-\,ik\hat{x}\cdot z_{1}}\,\hat{x}\times\tilde{Q}_{1}\right]

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\,\frac{k^{2}}{4\,\pi}\left[\,e^{-\,ik\hat{x}\cdot z_{1}}\,\left(I-\hat{x}\otimes\hat{x}\right)\cdot\tilde{R}_{1}\,+\,i\,k\,\,e^{-\,ik\hat{x}\cdot z_{2}}\,\hat{x}\times\tilde{Q}_{2}\right]\,+\,\mathcal{O}\left(a^{\min(3;7-2h-3t;10-2h-7t)}\right),

which, by using the expression of
\left(\tilde{R}_{1},\tilde{Q}_{2}\right)
in (
4.1
), and the fact that the second term on the R.H.S of the above equation is of order
\mathcal{O}\left(a^{3}\right)
, can be further simplified as

E^{\infty}(\hat{x})\,=\,\frac{k^{2}}{4\,\pi}\left[\,e^{-\,ik\hat{x}\cdot z_{2}}\,\left(I-\hat{x}\otimes\hat{x}\right)\cdot\tilde{R}_{2}\,+\,i\,k\,e^{-\,ik\hat{x}\cdot z_{1}}\,\hat{x}\times\tilde{Q}_{1}\right]\,+\,\mathcal{O}\left(a^{\min(3;7-2h-3t;10-2h-7t)}\right).

Now, taking Taylor expansion of
z\rightarrow e^{-\,i\,k\hat{x}\cdot z}
at the intermediate point
z_{0}
, between
z_{1}
and
z_{2}
with
\left|z_{1}-z_{2}\right|\,\lesssim\,a^{t}
, we obtain

\displaystyle E^{\infty}(\hat{x})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{k^{2}}{4\,\pi}\,e^{-\,ik\hat{x}\cdot z_{0}}\,\left[\,\left(I-\hat{x}\otimes\hat{x}\right)\cdot\tilde{R}_{2}\,+\,i\,k\,\hat{x}\times\tilde{Q}_{1}\right]

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}\left(a^{t}\;\left(\left|\tilde{R}_{2}\right|+\left|\tilde{Q}_{1}\right|\right)\right)\,+\,\mathcal{O}\left(a^{\min(3;7-2h-3t;10-2h-7t)}\right).

Now, using the expression of
\left(\tilde{R}_{2},\,\tilde{Q}_{1}\right)
in (
4.1
), we can derive the following estimation

\left|a^{t}\;\left(\left|R_{2}\right|+\left|Q_{1}\right|\right)\right|\;\lesssim\;a^{3-h+t},

and the following expression for
E^{\infty}(\cdot)
under the condition
4-h-4t>0
that,

\displaystyle E^{\infty}(\hat{x})\,

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,\pm\,\frac{k^{2}}{4\,\pi}\,a^{3-h}\,e^{-\,ik\hat{x}\cdot z_{0}}\,\left[\frac{\eta_{2}}{d_{0}}\,\left(I-\hat{x}\otimes\hat{x}\right)\cdot{\bf P}_{0,2}^{(2)}\cdot E_{2}^{Inc}(z_{0})\,-\,k^{2}\frac{\eta_{0}}{c_{0}}\,\hat{x}\times{\bf P}_{0,1}^{(1)}\cdot H_{1}^{Inc}(z_{0})\right]

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\,\mathcal{O}\left(a^{\min(3-h+t;\;3;\;10-2h-7t;\;9-3h-5t)}\right),

where the last step consists in taking Taylor expansion for the incident fields, similar to (
4.15
), to justify
(\ref{CoroFF})
. The proof of Corollary
1.2
is now complete.

5.
Proof of the a-priori estimates

There will be three subsections in this section, in which we respectively prove Lemma
2.4
, Proposition
2.5
and Proposition
2.7
.

5.1.
Proof of Lemma
2.4

(Estimate for just one nano-particle)
To justify the estimation related to the dielectric nano-particle, i.e. estimation of
\lVert\tilde{E}\rVert_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(B_{1})}
, we refer the readers to
[
9
, Subsection 5.1, Proof of Proposition 2.2]
for a detailed proof on the estimation of the
\mathbb{L}^{2}
-norm of the electric field generated by a single dielectric nano-particle embedded into a background made up of a vacuum. In this subsection, by following a similar arguments used in
[
9
, Subsection 5.1]
with some necessary slight changes, we investigate only the estimation of
\lVert\tilde{E}\rVert_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(B_{2})}
, i.e. we estimate the
\mathbb{L}^{2}
-norm of the electric field generated by a single plasmonic nano-particle embedded into a background of a vacuum. To do this, we start by recalling the L.S.E given by
(\ref{LS eq2})
,

E\,+\,\eta_{2}\,\nabla M^{k}_{D_{2}}(E)\,-\,k^{2}\,\eta_{2}\,N^{k}_{D_{2}}(E)\,=\,E^{Inc},\quad\text{in}\;\;D_{2},

which after scaling to
B_{2}
yields

(5.1)

\tilde{E}\,+\,\eta_{2}\,\nabla M^{ka}_{B_{2}}(\tilde{E})\,-\,k^{2}\,\eta_{2}\,a^{2}\,N^{ka}_{B_{2}}(\tilde{E})\,=\,\tilde{E}^{Inc},\quad\text{in}\;\;B_{2}.

The next step consists in projecting
\tilde{E}
onto the three sub-spaces introduced in (
2.1
). We only note the main results. The reader should refer to
[
9
, Subsection 5.1]
for the calculations details.

(1)

Projecting onto
\mathbb{H}_{0}(Curl=0)(B_{2})
. By using the fact that

\tilde{E}^{Inc}\in\mathbb{H}\left(\mathrm{div}\,=0\right)(B_{2})\,=\,\left(\mathbb{H}_{0}\left(\mathrm{div}\,=0\right)\oplus\nabla\mathcal{H}armonic\right)(B_{2})\perp\mathbb{H}_{0}\left(Curl=0\right)(B_{2}),

and the relation
(\ref{grad-M-2nd})
, in
(\ref{Equa1104})
, we can derive that
\left\langle\tilde{E},e_{n,B_{2}}^{(2)}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(B_{2})}\,=\,0
, hence

(5.2)

\overset{2}{\mathbb{P}}\left(\tilde{E}\right)\;=\;0,

which justifies (
2.9
).

(2)

Projecting onto
\mathbb{H}_{0}(\mathrm{div}\,=0)(B_{2})
. In
(\ref{Equa1104})
, by taking the inner product with respect to
e^{(1)}_{n,B_{2}}(\cdot)
, using
(\ref{grad-M-1st})
and
(\ref{Exact2ndproj})
, summing up with respect to the index
n
, we end up with the following estimation

(5.3)

\left\|\overset{1}{\mathbb{P}}\left(\tilde{E}\right)\right\|^{2}_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(B_{2})}\;\lesssim\;\left\lVert\overset{1}{\mathbb{P}}\left(\tilde{E}^{inc}\right)\right\rVert_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(B_{2})}^{2}+a^{8}\,\left\|\overset{3}{\mathbb{P}}\left(\tilde{E}\right)\right\|^{2}_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(B_{2})}.

(3)

Projecting onto
\nabla\mathcal{H}armonic(B_{2})
. In
(\ref{Equa1104})
, by taking the inner product with respect to
e^{(3)}_{n,B_{2}}(\cdot)
, using
(\ref{expansion-gradMk})
and
(\ref{expansion-Nk})
, and keeping only the dominant terms we can end up with the following approximation

\left\langle\tilde{E},e^{(3)}_{n,B_{2}}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(B_{2})}\,\simeq\,\frac{\left\langle\tilde{E}^{Inc},e^{(3)}_{n,B_{2}}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(B_{2})}}{\left(1+\eta_{2}\,\lambda^{(3)}_{n}(B_{2})\right)}+\frac{\eta_{2}}{\left(1+\eta_{2}\,\lambda^{(3)}_{n}(B_{2})\right)}\;\frac{\left(ka\right)^{2}}{2}\,\left\langle N_{B_{2}}\left(\tilde{E}\right),e^{(3)}_{n,B_{2}}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(B_{2})}.

Taking the square modulus on the both sides of the above equation, summing up with respect to the index
n
and using
(\ref{def-eta})
and (
1.11
), we deduce

(5.4)

\left\|\overset{3}{\mathbb{P}}\left(\tilde{E}\right)\right\|^{2}_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(B_{2})}\lesssim a^{-2h}\;\left|\left\langle\tilde{E}^{inc},e_{n_{*},B_{2}}^{(3)}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(B_{2})}\right|^{2}+\sum_{n\neq n_{*}}\frac{\left|\left\langle\tilde{E}^{inc},e_{n,B_{2}}^{(3)}\right\rangle\right|^{2}}{\left|1+\eta_{2}\lambda_{n}^{(3)}(B_{2})\right|^{2}}+a^{4-2h}\left\|\tilde{E}\right\|^{2}_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(B_{2})}.

Now, by adding
(\ref{12120111})
,
(\ref{Exact2ndproj})
and
(\ref{Hsah})
, under the condition
h\,<\,2
, we deduce

\left\|\tilde{E}\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(B_{2})}\;=\;\mathcal{O}\left(a^{-h}\right).

This ends the proof of Lemma
2.4
.

5.2.
Proof of Proposition
2.5

(Estimation for the dimer).
For shortness notation, we introduce the following integro-differential operator

(5.5)

\bm{T}_{k,m}\,:=\,\left(I\,+\,\eta_{m}\,\nabla M^{k}_{D_{m}}\,-\,k^{2}\,\eta_{m}\,N^{k}_{D_{m}}\right),\quad\mbox{for}\quad m=1,\,2.

Since the operators
\nabla M^{-k}_{D_{m}}(\cdot)
and
N^{-k}_{D_{m}}(\cdot)
are the adjoint operators of
\nabla M^{k}_{D_{m}}(\cdot)
and
N^{k}_{D_{m}}(\cdot)
, respectively, we deduce that
\bm{T}_{-k,m}(\cdot)
is the adjoint operator of
\bm{T}_{k,m}(\cdot)
. To justify the estimations given by
(\ref{max-P1P3})
, we start by recalling the L.S.E, given by (
2.3
),

(5.6)

\displaystyle E(x)\,

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\,\left(\eta_{1}\,\underset{x}{\nabla}M^{k}_{D_{1}}(E_{1})(x)\,+\,\eta_{2}\,\underset{x}{\nabla}M^{k}_{D_{2}}(E_{2})(x)\right)

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle k^{2}\,\left(\eta_{1}\,N^{k}_{D_{1}}(E_{1})(x)\,+\,\eta_{2}\,N^{k}_{D_{2}}(E_{2})(x)\right)\,=\,E^{Inc}(x),\quad x\in D.

Now, we estimate the following
\mathbb{L}^{2}
-norm,

\left\|\overset{j}{\mathbb{P}}\left(\tilde{E}_{m}\right)\right\|_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(B_{m})},\quad m=1,2\quad\text{and}\quad j=1,3.

(1)

Estimation of
\left\|\overset{1}{\mathbb{P}}\left(\tilde{E}_{1}\right)\right\|^{2}_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(B_{1})}
.

Let
x\in D_{1}
and rewrite
(\ref{LS-es})
as
3
3

3

As
x
and
y
are in two disjoint domains, the integral appearing on the left hand side of
(\ref{*add3})
is well defined.

(5.7)

\bm{T}_{k,1}(E_{1})(x)\,-\,\eta_{2}\,\left(-\nabla M^{k}_{D_{2}}\,+\,k^{2}\,N^{k}_{D_{2}}\right)\,\left(E_{2}\right)(x)\,=\,E^{Inc}_{1}(x),\quad x\in D_{1}.

Then, by using
(\ref{grad-M-1st})
, taking the inverse operator of
\bm{T}_{k,1}
on the both sides of
(\ref{*add3})
and using the fact that, for
m=1,2
,
E_{m}\,=\,\overset{1}{\mathbb{P}}\left(E_{m}\right)\,+\,\overset{3}{\mathbb{P}}\left(E_{m}\right)
, we get

(5.8)

\displaystyle\overset{1}{\mathbb{P}}\left(E_{1}\right)(x)

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\,k^{2}\,\eta_{2}\,\bm{T}_{k,1}^{-1}\int_{D_{2}}\Phi_{k}(x,y)\cdot\overset{1}{\mathbb{P}}\left(E_{2}\right)(y)\,dy

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bm{T}_{k,1}^{-1}\left(E^{Inc}_{1}\right)(x)-\overset{3}{\mathbb{P}}\left(E_{1}\right)(x)+\,\eta_{2}\,\bm{T}_{k,1}^{-1}\left(-\nabla M_{D_{2}}^{k}+k^{2}N_{D_{2}}^{k}\right)\left(\overset{3}{\mathbb{P}}\left(E_{2}\right)\right)(x),\quad x\in D_{1}.

Besides, we know that

\displaystyle\left(-\nabla M_{D_{2}}^{k}+k^{2}N_{D_{2}}^{k}\right)\left(\overset{3}{\mathbb{P}}(E_{2})\right)(x)\,

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,I_{2}^{0}(x)\,+\,\int_{D_{2}}\underset{y}{\nabla}\,\underset{y}{\nabla}\,\left(\Phi_{k}\,-\,\Phi_{0}\right)(x,y)\cdot\overset{3}{\mathbb{P}}(E_{2})(y)\,dy

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle k^{2}\int_{D_{2}}\Phi_{k}(x,y)\overset{3}{\mathbb{P}}(E_{2})(y)\,dy,

where

(5.9)

I_{2}^{0}(x)\,:=\,\int_{D_{2}}\underset{y}{\nabla}\,\underset{y}{\nabla}\,\Phi_{0}(x,y)\cdot\overset{3}{\mathbb{P}}(E_{2})(y)\,dy\,=\,-\,\nabla M_{D_{2}}\left(\overset{3}{\mathbb{P}}(E_{2})\right)(x),\qquad x\in D_{1}.

Then we can reformulate
(\ref{es-ls1})
as

(5.10)

\displaystyle\overset{1}{\mathbb{P}}(E_{1})(x)

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\eta_{2}k^{2}\bm{T}_{k,1}^{-1}\int_{D_{2}}\Phi_{k}(x,y)\overset{1}{\mathbb{P}}(E_{2})(y)\,dy

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bm{T}_{k,1}^{-1}(E_{1}^{Inc})(x)-\overset{3}{\mathbb{P}}(E_{1})(x)+\eta_{2}\bm{T}_{k,1}^{-1}(I_{2}^{0})

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\eta_{2}\bm{T}_{k,1}^{-1}\int_{D_{2}}\underset{y}{\nabla}\underset{y}{\nabla}\left(\Phi_{k}-\Phi_{0}\right)(x,y)\cdot\overset{3}{\mathbb{P}}(E_{2})(y)\,dy\,+\,\eta_{2}k^{2}\bm{T}_{k,1}^{-1}\int_{D_{2}}\Phi_{k}(x,y)\overset{3}{\mathbb{P}}(E_{2})(y)\,dy.

In (
5.10
), by taking the Taylor expansion of
\Phi_{k}(\cdot,\cdot)
and
\underset{y}{\nabla}\underset{y}{\nabla}(\Phi_{k}-\Phi_{0})(\cdot,\cdot)
respectively, we can get

(5.11)

\displaystyle\overset{1}{\mathbb{P}}\left(E_{1}\right)(x)

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\,\eta_{2}\,k^{2}\,\bm{T}_{k,1}^{-1}\underset{y}{\nabla}\left({\Phi_{k}}I\right)(z_{1},z_{2})\cdot\int_{D_{2}}\mathcal{P}(y,z_{2})\cdot\overset{1}{\mathbb{P}}(E_{2})(y)\,dy

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\eta_{2}\,k^{2}\,\bm{T}_{k,1}^{-1}\int_{D_{2}}\int_{0}^{1}(1-t)(y-z_{2})^{\perp}\cdot\underset{y}{\nabla}\underset{y}{\nabla}\left(\Phi_{k}\right)(z_{1},z_{2}+t(y-z_{2}))\cdot(y-z_{2})\,dt\cdot\overset{1}{\mathbb{P}}\left(E_{2}\right)(y)\;dy

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\eta_{2}\,k^{2}\,\bm{T}_{k,1}^{-1}\int_{D_{2}}\int_{0}^{1}\underset{y}{\nabla}\underset{y}{\nabla}\left(\Phi_{k}\right)(z_{1}+t(\cdot-z_{1}),z_{2})\cdot(\cdot-z_{1})\,dt\cdot\mathcal{P}(y,z_{2})\,\cdot\overset{1}{\mathbb{P}}\left(E_{2}\right)(y)\;dy

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\eta_{2}\,k^{2}\,\bm{T}_{k,1}^{-1}\int_{D_{2}}\int_{0}^{1}\int_{0}^{1}(1-t)(y-z_{2})^{\perp}\cdot\underset{x}{\nabla}(\underset{y}{\nabla}\underset{y}{\nabla}\Phi_{k})(z_{1}+s(\cdot-z_{1}),z_{2}+t(y-z_{2}))\cdot(y-z_{2})\,dt\cdot

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\cdot\mathcal{P}(\cdot,z_{1})\,ds\cdot\overset{1}{\mathbb{P}}(E_{2})(y)\,dy

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\bm{T}_{k,1}^{-1}\left(E^{Inc}_{1}\right)(x)-\overset{3}{\mathbb{P}}\left(E_{1}\right)(x)+\,\eta_{2}\,\bm{T}_{k,1}^{-1}(I_{2}^{0})(x)\,+\,\eta_{2}\,\bm{T}_{k,1}^{-1}\underset{y}{\nabla}\underset{y}{\nabla}(\Phi_{k}-\Phi_{0})(z_{1},z_{2})\cdot\int_{D_{2}}\overset{3}{\mathbb{P}}\left(E_{2}\right)(y)\,dy

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\eta_{2}\,\bm{T}_{k,1}^{-1}\int_{D_{2}}\int_{0}^{1}\underset{x}{\nabla}\left(\underset{y}{\nabla}\underset{y}{\nabla}(\Phi_{k}-\Phi_{0})\right)(z_{1}+t(\cdot-z_{1}),z_{2})\cdot\mathcal{P}(\cdot,z_{1})\,dt\cdot\overset{3}{\mathbb{P}}(E_{2})(y)\,dy

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\eta_{2}\,\bm{T}_{k,1}^{-1}\int_{D_{2}}\int_{0}^{1}\underset{y}{\nabla}\left(\underset{y}{\nabla}\underset{y}{\nabla}(\Phi_{k}-\Phi_{0})\right)(z_{1},z_{2}+t(y-z_{2}))\cdot\mathcal{P}(y,z_{2})\,dt\cdot\overset{3}{\mathbb{P}}(E_{2})(y)\,dy

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\eta_{2}\,k^{2}\,\bm{T}_{k,1}^{-1}(\Phi_{k}I)(z_{1},z_{2})\cdot\int_{D_{2}}\overset{3}{\mathbb{P}}\left(E_{2}\right)(y)\,dy

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\,\eta_{2}\,k^{2}\,\bm{T}_{k,1}^{-1}\int_{D_{2}}\int_{0}^{1}\underset{x}{\nabla}(\Phi_{k}I)(z_{1}+t(\cdot-z_{1}),z_{2})\cdot\mathcal{P}(\cdot,z_{1})\,dt\cdot\overset{3}{\mathbb{P}}(E_{2})(y)\,dy

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\,\eta_{2}\,k^{2}\,\bm{T}_{k,1}^{-1}\int_{D_{2}}\int_{0}^{1}\underset{y}{\nabla}(\Phi_{k}I)(z_{1},z_{2}+t(y-z_{2}))\mathcal{P}(y,z_{2})\,dt\cdot\overset{3}{\mathbb{P}}(E_{2})(y)\,dy.

By scaling the above equation, from
D_{m}
to
B_{m}
, with
m=1,2
, taking the
\mathbb{L}^{2}(B_{1})
-inner product with respect to
e_{n,B_{1}}^{(1)}(\cdot)
and with the help of the adjoint operator of
\bm{T}^{-1}_{ka,1}
, that
\left(\bm{T}^{-1}_{ka,1}\right)^{\star}=\bm{T}^{-1}_{-\,ka,1}
, we further have

(5.12)

\displaystyle\left\langle\tilde{E}_{1},e_{n,B_{1}}^{(1)}\right\rangle_{\mathbb{L}^{2}(B_{1})}

\displaystyle-
